0&\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[({\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}-{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}^{(i)})^{\intercal}({\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}-{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}^{(i)})|Z]\end{bmatrix}

We will use the following claim that we will prove later.

Claim 3.5
.

We have the following relations.

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[({\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}-{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}^{(i)})({\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}-{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}^{(i)})^{\intercal}|Z]=2(b+1){\mathbf{F}}_{a,b+1}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b+1}^{\intercal}

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[({\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}-{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}^{(i)})^{\intercal}({\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}-{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}^{(i)})|Z]=2(a+1){\mathbf{F}}_{a+1,b}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{F}}_{a+1,b}

This gives
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\tr\left[{\mathbf{V}}_{a,b}^{t}\right]=(b+1)^{t}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b+1}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+(a+1)^{t}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{F}}_{a+1,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}
. Therefore, we get

\displaystyle 2\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{X}}_{a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\tr\left[\overline{{\mathbf{X}}}_{a,b}^{2t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(2(2t-1))^{t}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\tr\left[{\mathbf{V}}_{a,b}^{t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(2(2t-1))^{t}((b+1)^{t}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b+1}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+(a+1)^{t}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{F}}_{a+1,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t})

\displaystyle\leq(2(2t-1))^{t}((b+1)^{t}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{X}}_{a,b+1}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b+1}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+(a+1)^{t}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{X}}_{a+1,b}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}_{a+1,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t})

\displaystyle\leq(16t)^{t}((b+1)^{t}(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{X}}_{a,b+1}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+\left\lVert\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b+1}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t})+(a+1)^{t}(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{X}}_{a+1,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+\left\lVert\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}_{a+1,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t})

\displaystyle\leq(16td_{p})^{t}(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{X}}_{a,b+1}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+\left\lVert\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b+1}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{X}}_{a+1,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+\left\lVert\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}_{a+1,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t})

It remains to prove the claim.

Proof of
3.5
.

We will prove the first equality. The second one is analogous.
For
I\in{\mathcal{I}},J\in{\mathcal{J}},\alpha,\beta\in\{0,1\}^{n}
, we have

({\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}-{\mathbf{F}}^{(i)}_{a,b})[(I,\alpha),(J,\beta)]=\begin{cases}\nabla_{\alpha+\beta}(f_{I,J}(Z)-f_{I,J}(Z^{(i)}))&\text{ if $|\alpha|_{0}=a,|\beta|_{0}=b,\alpha\cdot\beta=0$}\\
0&\text{o.w.}\end{cases}

By
3.4
, the first expression simplifies to
(Z_{i}-\widetilde{Z_{i}})\nabla_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\nabla_{\alpha+\beta}f_{I,J}(Z)
. Define the matrix
{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}
to be the matrix with the same set of rows and columns as
{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}
and whose only nonzero entries are given by

{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}[(I,\alpha),(J,\beta+\mathbf{e}_{i})]=\nabla_{\mathbf{e}_{i}}\nabla_{\alpha+\beta}f_{I,J}(Z)\text{ if $|\alpha|_{0}=a,|\beta|_{0}=b,\beta\cdot\mathbf{e}_{i}=0,\alpha\cdot(\beta+\mathbf{e}_{i})=0$}

Then, it’s easy to see that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}^{\intercal}=(b+1){\mathbf{F}}_{a,b+1}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b+1}^{T}
and
({\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}-{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}^{(i)})({\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}-{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}^{(i)})^{\intercal}=(Z-\widetilde{Z_{i}})^{2}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}^{\intercal}
. The latter equality implies

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[({\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}-{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}^{(i)})({\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}-{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}^{(i)})^{\intercal}|Z]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[(Z_{i}-\widetilde{Z_{i}})^{2}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}^{\intercal}|Z]=2{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}^{\intercal}

Therefore,

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[({\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}-{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}^{(i)})({\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}-{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b}^{(i)})^{\intercal}|Z]=2\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}^{\intercal}=2(b+1){\mathbf{F}}_{a,b+1}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b+1}^{\intercal}

4
Applications

To illustrate our framework, we apply it to obtain concentration bounds for nonlinear random matrices that have been considered in the literature before. The first one is a simple tensor network that arose in the analysis of spectral algorithms for a variant of principal components analysis (PCA)
[
hopkins2015tensor
,
hopkins2018statistical
]
.
The second application is to obtain norm bounds on dense graph matrices
[
medarametla2016bounds
,
ahn2016graph
]
. In the second application, the norm bounds are governed by a combinatorial structure called
the minimum vertex separator of a shape
. We will see how this notion arises naturally under our framework, while prior works that derived such bounds used the trace power method and required nontrivial combinatorial insights.

1
A simple tensor network

We consider the following result from
[
hopkins2015tensor
,
hopkins2018statistical
]
.

Lemma 4.1

(
[
hopkins2018statistical
]
, Theorem 6.7.1)
.

Let
c\in\{1,2\}
and let
d\geq 1
be an integer. Let
{\mathbf{A}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathbf{A}}_{n^{c}}
be i.i.d. random matrices uniformly sampled from
\{-1,1\}^{n^{d}\times n^{d}}
. Then, with probability
1-O(n^{-100})
,

\left\lVert\sum_{k\leq n^{c}}{\mathbf{A}}_{k}\otimes{\mathbf{A}}_{k}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\sum_{k\leq n^{c}}{\mathbf{A}}_{k}\otimes{\mathbf{A}}_{k}\right\rVert\leq C\sqrt{d}n^{(2d+c)/2}(\log n)^{1/2}

for an absolute constant
C>0
.

Using our framework, we will prove a slightly relaxed version of the inequality where
\sqrt{d}(\log n)^{1/2}
is replaced by
\log n
.
We remark that we have not attempted to optimize these extra factors in front of the dominating term
n^{(2d+c)/2}
, so it’s plausible that a more careful analysis can obtain a slightly better bound.

Proof of the relaxed bound.

Let the
i,j
-th entry of
{\mathbf{A}}_{k}
be
a_{k,i,j}
.
Let
{\mathbf{F}}=\sum_{i\leq n^{c}}{\mathbf{A}}_{k}\otimes{\mathbf{A}}_{k}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\sum_{i\leq n^{c}}{\mathbf{A}}_{k}\otimes{\mathbf{A}}_{k}
be a random matrix on the variables
a_{k,i,j}
for
k\leq n^{c},i,j\leq n^{d}
. So
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}=0
and we are looking for bounds on
\left\lVert{\mathbf{F}}\right\rVert
. The entries are given by

{\mathbf{F}}[(i_{1},i_{2}),(j_{1},j_{2})]=\begin{cases}\sum_{k\leq n^{c}}a_{k,i_{1},j_{1}}a_{k,i_{2},j_{2}}&\text{ if $(i_{1},j_{1})\neq(i_{2},j_{2})$}\\
0&\text{ if $(i_{1},j_{1})=(i_{2},j_{2})$}\end{cases}

The nonzero entries are homogeneous polynomials of degree
2
. Using
Theorem

1.2
,

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{F}}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}\leq(32t)^{2t}(\left\lVert\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}_{2,0}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+\left\lVert\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}_{1,1}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+\left\lVert\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}_{0,2}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t})

We will consider each of these terms.
In the following arguments, we restrict attention to indices
i_{1},i_{2},j_{1},j_{2}
such that
(i_{1},j_{1})\neq(i_{2},j_{2})
.

1.

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}_{2,0}
has nonzero entries in row
((i_{1},i_{2}),\{(k,i_{1},j_{1}),(k,i_{2},j_{2})\})
and column
(j_{1},j_{2})
and all these entries are
1
. The Schatten norm does not change when we permute the rows and columns. So, we can group the rows on
k,i_{1},i_{2}
and within each group, we can sort
j_{1},j_{2}
in both rows and columns. We get a matrix having
n^{2d+c}
identity matrices, each of dimensions
n^{2d}\times n^{2d}
, stacked on top of each other. Using the definition, the Schatten-
2t
norm of this matrix is easily computed to be
\left\lVert\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}_{2,0}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}=n^{c+4d}n^{t(2d+c)}
.

2.

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}_{1,1}
has nonzero entries in either row
((i_{1},i_{2}),\{(k,i_{1},j_{1})\})
and column
((j_{1},j_{2})
,
\{(k,i_{2},j_{2})\})
; or row
((i_{1},i_{2}),\{(k,i_{2},j_{2})\})
and column
((j_{1},j_{2}),\{(k,i_{1},j_{1})\})
and all these entries are
1
. So we can write
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}_{1,1}={\mathbf{A}}+{\mathbf{B}}
corresponding to the 2 sets of entries. Arguing just as in the previous case, we can obtain
\left\lVert{\mathbf{A}}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}=n^{c+4d}n^{t(2d+c)}
where we group the rows on
k,i_{2},j_{1}
and
\left\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}=n^{c+4d}n^{t(2d+c)}
where we group the rows on
k,i_{1},j_{2}
.
Therefore,
\left\lVert\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}_{1,1}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}\leq 2^{2t}(\left\lVert{\mathbf{A}}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+\left\lVert{\mathbf{B}}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t})=2^{2t+1}n^{c+4d}n^{t(2d+c)}
.

3.

The case
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}_{0,2}
is identical to
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}_{2,0}
.

Putting them together,
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{F}}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}\leq(C^{\prime}t)^{2t}n^{c+4d}n^{t(2d+c)}

for an absolute constant
C^{\prime}>0
. Now, we apply Markov’s inequality to get

\displaystyle Pr[\left\lVert{\mathbf{F}}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}\right\rVert\geq\theta]~\leq~Pr[\left\lVert{\mathbf{F}}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}\geq\theta^{2t}]

\displaystyle~\leq~\theta^{-2t}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{F}}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

\displaystyle~\leq~\theta^{-2t}(C^{\prime}t)^{2t}n^{c+4d}n^{t(2d+c)}

We now set
\theta=\varepsilon^{-1/(2t)}(C^{\prime}t)n^{(c+4d)/t}n^{(2d+c)/2}
to make this expression at most
\varepsilon
. Plug in
\varepsilon=n^{-100}
and set
t=\log n
to obtain that
\left\lVert{\mathbf{F}}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}\right\rVert\leq Cn^{(2d+c)/2}\log n
holds with probability
1-n^{-100}
, where
C>0
is an absolute constant.

2
Graph matrices

In this section, we first define graph matrices and then show how to obtain norm bounds for
dense graph matrices
, i.e. the case when
G\sim{\mathcal{G}}_{n,1/2}
, using our framework. Handling
sparse graph matrices
, i.e. the case when
G\sim{\mathcal{G}}_{n,p}
for
p=o(1)
, may not work well with our basic framework as we will explain in
Section

5
. Instead, our general framework in
Section

6
will handle this case well and we obtain sparse graph matrix norm bounds in
Section

8
.

Definitions

Define by
{\mathcal{G}}_{n,p}
the Erdős-Rényi random graph on the vertex set
[n]
with
n
vertices, where each edge is present independently with probability
p
. Let the graph be encoded by variables
G_{i,j}\in\Omega=\{-\sqrt{\frac{1-p}{p}},\sqrt{\frac{p}{1-p}}\}
where
-\sqrt{\frac{1-p}{p}}
indicates the presence of the edge
\{i,j\}
and
\sqrt{\frac{p}{1-p}}
indicates absence, for all
1\leq i,j\leq n
.

So, each
G_{i,j}
for
i<j
is sampled from
\Omega
where
G_{i,j}
takes the value
-\sqrt{\frac{1-p}{p}}
with probability
p
and takes the value
\sqrt{\frac{p}{1-p}}
otherwise. Here,
\Omega
has been normalized so that
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\Omega}[x]=0,\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{x\sim\Omega}[x^{2}]=1
. as is standard in
p
-biased Fourier analysis.

When
p=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
, we are in the setting of
dense graph matrices
. Then,
{\mathcal{G}}_{n,1/2}
can be thought of as a sampling of the
G_{i,j},i<j
independently and uniformly from
\Omega=\{-1,1\}
.

For a set of edges
E\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}
, define
G_{E}:=\prod_{e\in E}G_{e}
. When
p=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
, the
G_{E}
correspond to the Fourier basis for functions of the graph.

Define
{\mathcal{I}}
to be the set of sub-tuples of
[n]
, including the empty tuple. Graph matrices will have rows and columns indexed by
{\mathcal{I}}
. Each graph matrix has a succinct representation as a graph with some extra information, that is called a
shape
.

Definition 4.2

(Shape)
.

A shape is a tuple
\tau=(V(\tau),E(\tau),U_{\tau},V_{\tau})
where
(V(\tau),E(\tau))
is a graph and
U_{\tau},V_{\tau}
are ordered subsets of the vertices.

Definition 4.3

(Realization)
.

Given a shape
\tau
, a realization of
\tau
is an injective map
\varphi:V(\tau)\rightarrow[n].

Definition 4.4

(Graph matrices)
.

Let
\tau
be a shape.
Corresponding to
\tau
, the graph matrix
\mathbf{M}_{\tau}:\{\pm 1\}^{n\choose 2}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}^{{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathcal{I}}}
is defined to be the matrix-valued function with
I,J
-th entry defined as follows.

{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}[I,J]:=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Realization }\varphi\\
\varphi(U_{\tau})=I,\varphi(V_{\tau})=J\end{subarray}}{G_{\varphi(E(\tau))}}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{Realization }\varphi\\
\varphi(U_{\tau})=I,\varphi(V_{\tau})=J\end{subarray}}\prod_{(u,v)\in E(\tau)}G_{\varphi(u),\varphi(v)}

In other words, we sum over all realizations of
\tau
that map
U_{\tau},V_{\tau}
to
I,J
respectively and for each such realization, we have a term corresponding to the Fourier character that the realization gives.

Figure 3
:
Left: Shape corresponding to adjacency matrix, Right: Example of a more complicated shape

The following examples illustrate some simple graph matrices.

Example 4.5

(Adjacency matrix)
.

Let
\tau
be the shape on the left in
Fig.

3
, with two vertices
V(\tau)=\{u,v\}
and a single edge
E(\tau)=\{\{u,v\}\}
.
U_{\tau},V_{\tau}
are
(u),(v)
respectively where we use tuples to indicate ordering.
Then
{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}
has nonzero entries
{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}[(i),(j)](G)=G_{i,j}
for all
i\neq j
.
If
G\in\{\pm 1\}^{n\choose 2}
is thought of as a graph, then
{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}
has as principal submatrix the
\pm 1
adjacency matrix of
G
with zeros on the diagonal, and the other entries are
0
.

Example 4.6
.

In
Fig.

3
, consider the shape
\tau
on the right. We have
U_{\tau}=(u_{1},u_{2}),V_{\tau}=(v_{1}),V(\tau)=\{u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},w_{1}\}
and
E(\tau)=\{\{u_{1},w_{1}\},\{u_{2},w_{1}\},\{w_{1},v_{1}\}\}
.
{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}
is a matrix with rows and columns indexed by sub-tuples of
[n]
. Its nonzero entries are in rows
I
and columns
J
with
|I|=|U_{\tau}|=2
and
|J|=|V_{\tau}|=1
respectively. More specifically, for all distinct
a_{1},a_{2},b_{1}
, the entry corresponding to row
(a_{1},a_{2})
and column
(b_{1})
is
\sum_{c_{1}\in[n]\setminus\{a_{1},a_{2},b_{1}\}}G_{a_{1},c_{1}}G_{a_{2},c_{1}}G_{c_{1},b_{1}}
.
Here, each term is obtained via the realization
\varphi
that maps
u_{1},u_{2},w_{1},v_{1}
to
a_{1},a_{2},c_{1},b_{1}
respectively. Succinctly,

{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}=\begin{array}[]{rl@{}c@{}r}&&\makebox[0.0pt]{column $(b_{1})$}\\[-2.15277pt]
&&\,\downarrow\\[-2.15277pt]
&&\vdots&\\[-0.86108pt]
\text{row }(a_{1},a_{2})\rightarrow&\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\makebox[32.71089pt][l]{\leaders{\hbox{.}}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}}&\sum_{c_{1}\in[n]\setminus\{a_{1},a_{2},b_{1}\}}G_{a_{1},c_{1}}G_{a_{2},c_{1}}G_{c_{1},b_{1}}&\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\makebox[42.93306pt][r]{\leaders{\hbox{.}}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}}\\[2.15277pt]
&&\vdots&\\
\end{array}

Intuitively, graph matrices are symmetrizations of the Fourier basis, where the symmetry is incorporated by summing over all realizations of “free” vertices
V(\tau)\setminus U_{\tau}\setminus V_{\tau}
of the shape
\tau
.
For more examples of graph matrices and why they can be a useful tool to work with, see
[
ahn2016graph
]
.

Norm bounds for dense graph matrices

In this section, we study the concentration of the so-called “dense graph matrices” which is a term that refers to graph matrices
M_{\tau}
in the setting
p=\nicefrac{{1}}{{2}}
.
Since the edges of a random graph sampled from
{\mathcal{G}}_{n,1/2}
can be viewed as independent Rademacher random variables, we can apply our framework in this setting.

In particular, we will obtain bounds on
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}
.
The
G_{i,j}\in\{-1,1\}
correspond to the
Z_{i}
s in
Section

3
and for a fixed shape
\tau
,
{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}
will be the matrix
{\mathbf{F}}
we are interested in analyzing. For
I,J\in{\mathcal{I}}
,
{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}[I,J]
is a nonzero polynomial only when there exists at least one realization of
\tau
that maps
U_{\tau},V_{\tau}
to
I,J
respectively. In particular, we must have
|I|=|U_{\tau}|
and
|J|=|V_{\tau}|
. In this case,
{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}[I,J]
is a homogenous polynomial of degree
|E(\tau)|
.

By
Theorem

1.2
, we have

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}~\leq~\sum_{a+b\geq 1\atop a,b\geq 0}(16t|E(\tau)|)^{(a+b)t}\left\lVert\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

where for integers
a,b\geq 0
,
{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b}
is defined to be the matrix with rows and columns each indexed by
{\mathcal{I}}\times\{0,1\}^{\binom{n}{2}}
such that for all
I,J\in{\mathcal{I}}
, we have

{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b}[(I,\alpha),(J,\beta)]~=~\begin{cases}\nabla_{\alpha+\beta}{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}[I,J]&\text{ if $|\alpha|_{0}=a,|\beta|_{0}=b,\alpha\cdot\beta=0$}\\
0&\text{o.w.}\end{cases}

For any multilinear homogenous polynomial
f
of degree
d
, since
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[G_{i,j}]=0
for all
i,j
, we have
\nabla_{\alpha}f=0
whenever
|\alpha|_{0}<d
. Therefore,
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b}=0
for all
a+b<|E(G)|
. Moreover,
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b}=0
whenever
a+b\neq|E(G)|
otherwise
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b}={\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b}
. So, we can further simplify the above expression to

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}~\leq~\sum_{a+b=|E(\tau)|\atop a,b\geq 0}(16t|E(\tau)|)^{|E(\tau)|t}\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

It remains to analyze
\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}
for
a+b=|E(G)|
. We will see that analyzing these matrices is much simpler since they are deterministic matrices and simple computations using the Frobenius norm bound will work well. To state our final bounds, we need to define the notion of vertex separators of shapes.

Remark 4.7
.

As we will see, when analyzing the Frobenius norms for these deterministic matrices, the notion of the minimum vertex separator arises naturally. In prior trace method calculations (e.g.
[
medarametla2016bounds
]
,
[
ahn2016graph
]
), this required ingenious combinatorial observations.

Definition 4.8

(Vertex separator)
.

For a shape
\tau
, define a vertex separator to be a subset of vertices
S\subseteq V(\tau)
such that there is no path from
U_{\tau}
to
V_{\tau}
in
\tau\setminus S
, which is the shape obtained by deleting all the vertices of
S
(including all edges they’re incident on).

For a shape
\tau
, denote by
S_{\tau}
a vertex separator of the smallest size. Also, let
I_{\tau}
be the set of isolated vertices (vertices with degree
0
) in
V(\tau)\setminus U_{\tau}\setminus V_{\tau}
, so the presence of these vertices essentially scale the matrix by a scalar factor.

Theorem 4.9
.

For a shape
\tau
and any integer
t\geq 1
,

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}\leq\bigg(C^{t|E(\tau)|}n^{|V(\tau)|}t^{t|E(\tau)|}|E(\tau)|^{2t|E(\tau)|}\bigg)n^{t(|V(\tau)|-|S_{\tau}|+|I_{\tau}|)}

for an absolute constant
C>0
.

Up to lower order terms, the same result has been shown before in
[
medarametla2016bounds
,
ahn2016graph
]
. To interpret this bound, assume that
\tau
has a constant number of vertices. By setting
t\approx{\mathrm{polylog}}(n)
, we get

\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}\right\rVert=\widetilde{\operatorname{O}}\left(\sqrt{n}^{|V(\tau)|-|S_{\tau}|+|I_{\tau}|}\right)

with high probability, where
\widetilde{\operatorname{O}}
hides logarithmic factors.
This is obtained by applying Markov’s inequality on the bound on
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}
. If
\tau
has at least one edge, then
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}=0
and
Theorem

4.9
yields such bounds. If
\tau
has no edges, then it’s quite simple to obtain such a bound and we include it in
Lemma

4.10
for the sake of completeness.
Corollary

4.11
makes precise the high probability bound above. Therefore, this power of
n
is essentially what controls the norm bound and this is utilized heavily in applications (e.g.
[
BHKKMP16
,
ghosh2020sum
,
potechin2020machinery
]
).

Proof of
Theorem

4.9
.

We first argue that we can assume
I_{\tau}=\emptyset
. This is because of the following reason. Each distinct vertex in
\tau
of degree
0
essentially scales the matrix by a factor of at most
n
. And in the right hand side of the inequality, each vertex in
I_{\tau}
contributes a factor of
n^{2t}
accordingly, from
n^{t|V(\tau)|}
and from
n^{t|I_{\tau}|}
, and the other changes only weaken the inequality.

Now, fix
a,b\geq 0
such that
a+b=|E(\tau)|
and consider
{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b}
. For
I,J\in{\mathcal{I}},\alpha,\beta\in\{0,1\}^{\binom{n}{2}}
such that
|\alpha|_{0}=a,|\beta|_{0}=b,\alpha\cdot\beta=0
, by definition,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b}[(I,\alpha),(J,\beta)]

\displaystyle~=~\nabla_{\alpha+\beta}\left(\sum_{\varphi:\varphi(U_{\tau})=I,\varphi(V_{\tau})=J}\prod_{u,v\in E(\tau)}G_{\varphi(u),\varphi(v)}\right)

\displaystyle~=~|\{\varphi~|~\varphi(U_{\tau})=I,\varphi(V_{\tau})=J,\varphi(E(\tau))=\supp(\alpha+\beta)\}|

where
\supp(.)
denotes the support. We will now obtain norm bounds on these deterministic matrices by reinterpreting them as graph matrices for different shapes.

Let
P=(E_{1},E_{2})
denote the partition of
E(\tau)=E_{1}\sqcup E_{2}
into two ordered sets
E_{1},E_{2}
, where
\sqcup
denotes disjoint union. Then, we can write
{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b}=\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b,P}
where

{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b,P}[(I,\alpha),(J,\beta)]~=~|\{\varphi~|~\varphi(U_{\tau})=I,\varphi(V_{\tau})=J,\varphi(E_{1})=\supp(\alpha),\varphi(E_{2})=\supp(\beta)\}|

Let the set of ordered partitions
P
be
{\mathcal{P}}
. Then,
|{\mathcal{P}}|\leq(4|E(\tau)|)^{|E(\tau)|}
and so, by
2.3
,

\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}\leq(4|E(\tau)|)^{t|E(\tau)|}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b,P}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

Each
{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b,P}
can be interpreted as a graph matrix for a different shape
\tau_{P}
, with the same vertex set and no edges. Let
V(\tau_{P})=V(\tau),E(\tau_{P})=\emptyset
and set
U_{\tau_{P}}=U_{\tau}\cup V(E_{1}),V(\tau_{P})=V_{\tau}\cup V(E_{2})
using a canonical ordering. Then,
{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b}
is equal to
{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau_{P}}
up to renaming of the rows and columns. For an illustration, see
Fig.

4
.

Figure 4
:
An example illustrating how
\tau_{P}
is defined. In this example,
P
constraints the blue and red edges to go to
\alpha
and
\beta
respectively.
U_{\tau_{P}},V_{\tau_{P}}
have an ordering on the vertices (not shown here).

This graph matrix has a block diagonal structure indexed by the realizations of the set of common vertices
S=U_{\tau_{P}}\cap V_{\tau_{P}}
. Indeed, for
K\in[n]^{S}
, let
{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau_{P},K}
be the block of
{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau_{P}}
with
\varphi(S)=K
. Then,
{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau_{P},K}{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau_{P},K^{\prime}}^{\intercal}={\mathbf{M}}_{\tau_{P},K}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau_{P},K^{\prime}}=0
for
K\neq K^{\prime}
and so,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

\displaystyle\leq(4|E(\tau)|)^{t|E(\tau)|}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau_{P}}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

\displaystyle=(4|E(\tau)|)^{t|E(\tau)|}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\sum_{T\in[n]^{S}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau_{P},T}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

\displaystyle\leq(4|E(\tau)|)^{t|E(\tau)|}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\sum_{T\in[n]^{S}}\left(\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau_{P},T}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\right)^{t}

where we bounded the Schatten norm by the appropriate power of the Frobenius norm.

For any fixed
K\in[n]^{S}
, the entries of
{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau_{P},K}
take values in
\{0,1\}
and the number of nonzero entries is at most
n^{|V(\tau)|-|S|}
because the realizations of vertices in
S
are fixed and the other vertices have at most
n
choices each. Therefore,
\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau_{P},K}\right\rVert_{2}^{2}\leq n^{|V(\tau)|-|S|}
.

Finally, we bound
|S|
to estimate how large this term can be over all possibilities of
P
.
We argue that
S
blocks all paths from
U_{\tau}
to
V_{\tau}
. To see this, consider any path from
U_{\tau}
to
V_{\tau}
, it must contain an edge
(u,v)\in E(\tau)
such that
u\in U_{\tau_{P}},v\in V_{\tau_{P}}
. We must either have
(u,v)\in E_{1}
, in which case
u,v\in U_{\tau_{P}}
and
v\in S
, or
(u,v)\in E_{2}
, in which case
u,v\in V_{\tau_{P}}
and
u\in S
. In either case,
S
must contain either
u
or
v
. This argument implies
S
must be a vertex separator of
\tau
, giving
|S|\geq|S_{\tau}|
.
For a proof by picture, see
Fig.

5
.

Figure 5
:
Proof by picture that
|S|\geq|S_{\tau}|
. Green edges can occur in
\tau
, orange edges cannot, so
S
blocks all paths from
U_{\tau}
to
V_{\tau}
.

We also have the trivial upper bound
|S|\leq|V(\tau)|
. Ultimately, this gives

\displaystyle\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

\displaystyle\leq(4|E(\tau)|)^{t|E(\tau)|}\sum_{P\in{\mathcal{P}}}\sum_{T\in[n]^{S}}n^{t(|V(\tau)|-|S_{\tau}|)}

\displaystyle\leq(4|E(\tau)|)^{t|E(\tau)|}(4|E(\tau)|)^{|E(\tau)|}n^{|V(\tau)|}n^{t(|V(\tau)|-|S_{\tau}|)}

Along with our prior discussion, we get

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{a+b=|E(\tau)|}(16t|E(\tau)|)^{|E(\tau)|t}\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{a+b=|E(\tau)|}(16t|E(\tau)|)^{|E(\tau)|t}(4|E(\tau)|)^{t|E(\tau)|}(4|E(\tau)|)^{|E(\tau)|}n^{|V(\tau)|}n^{t(|V(\tau)|-|S_{\tau}|)}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg(C^{t|E(\tau)|}n^{|V(\tau)|}t^{t|E(\tau)|}|E(\tau)|^{2t|E(\tau)|}\bigg)n^{t(|V(\tau)|-|S_{\tau}|)}

for an absolute constant
C>0
.

In the proof above, our analysis of the shape
\tau_{P}
which has no edges, applies in general to any shape
\tau
with no edges. For the sake of completeness, we state it explicity in the following lemma.

Lemma 4.10
.

For a shape
\tau
with no edges and any integer
t\geq 1
,

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}\leq n^{|U_{\tau}\cap V_{\tau}|}n^{t(V(\tau)-|U_{\tau}\cap V_{\tau}|+|I_{\tau}|)}

Note that this has the same form as
Theorem

4.9
because for a shape
\tau
with no edges, the minimum vertex separator
S_{\tau}
is just
U_{\tau}\cap V_{\tau}
.

The following corollary obtains high probability norm bounds for norms of graph matrices via Markov’s inequality.

Corollary 4.11
.

For a shape
\tau
, for any constant
\varepsilon>0
, with probability
1-\varepsilon
,

\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}\right\rVert\leq(C|E(\tau)|\log(n^{|V(\tau)|}/\varepsilon))^{|E(\tau)|}\cdot\sqrt{n}^{|V(\tau)|-|S_{\tau}|+|I_{\tau}|}

for an absolute constant
C>0
.

Proof.

If
E(\tau)=\emptyset
, we invoke
Lemma

4.10
. Otherwise,
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}=0
and we invoke
Theorem

4.9
. By an application of Markov’s inequality,

\displaystyle Pr[\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}\right\rVert\geq\theta]

\displaystyle\leq Pr[\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}\geq\theta^{2t}]

\displaystyle\leq\theta^{-2t}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{M}}_{\tau}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

\displaystyle\leq\theta^{-2t}\bigg((C^{\prime})^{t|E(\tau)|}n^{|V(\tau)|}t^{t|E(\tau)|}|E(\tau)|^{2t|E(\tau)|}\bigg)n^{t(|V(\tau)|-|S_{\tau}|+|I_{\tau}|)}

for an absolute constant
C^{\prime}>0
. We now set

\theta=\bigg(\varepsilon^{-1/(2t)}(C^{\prime\prime})^{|E(\tau)|}n^{|V(\tau)|/(2t)}t^{|E(\tau)|/2}|E(\tau)|^{|E(\tau)|}\bigg)\sqrt{n}^{|V(\tau)|-|S_{\tau}|+|I_{\tau}|}

for an absolute constant
C^{\prime\prime}>0
, to make this expression at most
\varepsilon
. Set
t=\frac{1}{2}\log(n^{|V(\tau)|}/\varepsilon)
to complete the proof.

5
Why a naïve application of
[
paulin2016
]
may fail for general product distributions

In this section, we elaborate on the difficulties that arise when working with random variables that are not necessarily Rademacher. In this case, note that we cannot assume that the polynomial entries are multilinear as well.

To recall the setting, we are given a random matrix
{\mathbf{F}}
whose entries are low degree polynomials in random variables
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}
which are independently sampled from arbitrary distributions. And we wish to obtain concentration bounds on how much
{\mathbf{F}}
can deviate from its mean, by way of controlling
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{F}}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}
.

Building on the ideas from
Section

3
, we could attempt to use matrix Efron-Stein,
Theorem

1.1
and hope to obtain a similar recursion framework. We now discuss what happens if we do this. Assume
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{i}]=0,\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{i}^{2}]=1
. We can proceed similar to the proof of
Theorem

1.2
. So, we consider
{\mathbf{X}}
as a principal submatrix of
{\mathbf{X}}_{0,0}
and follow through
Lemma

3.3
. The main change will happen in
3.5
. In particular, the equation
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[(Z_{i}-\widetilde{Z_{i}})^{2}|Z]=2
is no longer true. Instead, we will have
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[(Z_{i}-\widetilde{Z_{i}})^{2}|Z]=1+Z_{i}^{2}
. So, we get the expression

\sum_{i=1}^{n}(1+Z_{i}^{2}){\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}^{\intercal}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}^{\intercal}+\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}^{2}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}^{\intercal}

The first term can been handled just as in the basic framework. Unfortunately, the second term will be a source of difficulty. To get around this difficulty, we could attempt to apply the matrix Efron-Stein inequality again on an appropriately constructed matrix.
To do this, we can interpret the second term as having been obtained after differentiating with respect to the variable
Z_{i}
and then
putting the variable back
. In contrast, we didn’t need to put it back when working with Rademacher random variables.
But after we do this, when we recurse on these extra matrices, the new second term will contain the left hand side as a sub-term, thereby giving a trivial inequality and stalling the recursion.

To see this more clearly, consider the simplest case
a=b=0
. Then, the first term
\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}^{\intercal}
will be equal to
{\mathbf{F}}_{0,1}{\mathbf{F}}_{0,1}^{\intercal}
as we saw earlier. To evaluate the second term
\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}^{2}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}{\mathbf{F}}_{a,b,i}^{\intercal}
in a similar manner, we define the matrix
{\mathbf{H}}
to be the same as
{\mathbf{F}}_{0,1}
except that each entry is now multiplied by
Z_{i}
where
i
is the differentiated variable in the column. That is,
{\mathbf{H}}[I,(J,\mathbf{e}_{i})]=Z_{i}{\mathbf{F}}_{0,1}[I,(J,\mathbf{e}_{i})]
. Observe that in the definition of
{\mathbf{H}}
,
Z_{i}
has been put back after differentiating with respect to it. Then, the second term will be
{\mathbf{H}}{\mathbf{H}}^{\intercal}
and we can hope to use Efron-Stein again on this matrix
{\mathbf{H}}
recursively.

We could do that and proceed similarly to the proof of
Lemma

3.3
with appropriate modifications as above. But since
\beta_{i}=1
already, differentiating with respect to
Z_{i}
and putting it back, will return the same matrix
{\mathbf{H}}
! So, we end up with an inequality of the form

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{H}}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}\leq\operatorname{O}(t)^{t}(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{H}}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+\text{ other nonnegative terms})

Indeed, this is a tautology and will not be useful to us.

For a quick and dirty bound, suppose we had a parameter
L
such that
1+Z_{i}^{2}\leq L
for our distributions, then we will be able to obtain a similar framework while incurring a loss of
\sqrt{L}
at each step of the recursion. But unfortunately, this bound will be lossy. For example, if we do this computation for the centered normalized adjacency matrix of
G\sim{\mathcal{G}}_{n,p}
, we will obtain a norm bound of
\widetilde{\operatorname{O}}(\frac{\sqrt{n(1-p)}}{\sqrt{p}})
where
\widetilde{\operatorname{O}}
hides logarithmic factors.. This bound is tight for constant or even inverse polylogarithmic
p
. But for
p=n^{-\theta}
for some constant
0<\theta<1
, this is not tight because in this regime, the true norm bound is known to be
\widetilde{\operatorname{O}}(\sqrt{n})
(see the early works of
[
furedi1981eigenvalues
,
vu2005spectral
]
and for tighter bounds, see
[
benaych2020spectral
]
and references therein).

If we dig into the details of what happened, this example illustrates that the matrix Efron-Stein inequality
Theorem

1.1
becomes a tautology for certain kinds of matrices, that yield
{\mathbf{V}}=\operatorname{O}(1){\mathbf{X}}{\mathbf{X}}^{\intercal}+\text{ other positive semidefinite matrices}
.

But in our framework in general, the aforementioned bad matrices occur when we differentiate with respect to variables that have already been differentiated on. In other words, the current definition of the variance proxy
{\mathbf{V}}
doesn’t take into account whether we have already differentiated with respect to some variable
Z_{i}
. So, for the general recursion, we dive into the proof due to
[
paulin2016
]
and modify it using structural properties of the intermediate matrices we obtain in our framework.

6
The general recursion framework

We now assume
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}
are i.i.d. random variables sampled from a distribution
\Omega
with finite moments.
We assume that they are identically distributed for simplicity but our technique easily extends even when they are not identically distributed, as long as they are independent.
For each
i\leq n
, define
\widetilde{Z_{i}}
to be an independent copy of
Z_{i}
and define the vector
Z^{(i)}:=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{i-1},\widetilde{Z_{i}},Z_{i+1},\ldots,Z_{n})
. Define
Z^{\prime}
to be the random vector defined by sampling
i
from
[n]
uniformly at random and then setting
Z^{\prime}=Z^{(i)}
.

Let
{\mathbf{F}}\in\mathbb{R}[Z]^{{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathcal{J}}}
be a matrix with rows and columns indexed by arbitrary sets
{\mathcal{I}},{\mathcal{J}}
respectively such that for all
I\in{\mathcal{I}},J\in{\mathcal{J}}
,
{\mathbf{F}}[I,J]
are polynomials of
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}
. Let
d_{p}
the maximum degree of
{\mathbf{F}}[I,J]
over all entries
I,J
and let
d
be the maximum degree of
Z_{i}
over all entries
{\mathbf{F}}[I,J]
and
i\leq n
.

Similar to the Rademacher case, let
{\mathbf{X}}:={\mathbf{F}}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}
. When the input is
Z
, we denote the matrices as
{\mathbf{F}},{\mathbf{X}}
, etc and when the input is
Z^{(i)}
, denote the corresponding matrices as
{\mathbf{F}}^{(i)},{\mathbf{X}}^{(i)}
, etc. In this section, we will give a general framework using which we can obtain bounds on
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{F}}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}
for any integer
t\geq 1
.

We set up a few preliminaries in order to state the main theorem.

Definition 6.1

(Space
{\mathcal{S}}
)
.

Let
{\mathcal{S}}
be the space of mean-zero polynomials in
Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n}
of degree at most
d_{p}
.

For
\alpha\neq 0
, we also define the centered monomials

\chi_{\alpha}(Z)=\prod_{\alpha_{i}>0}(Z_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{i}^{\alpha_{i}}])

By definition,
\chi_{\alpha}\in{\mathcal{S}}
for all
\alpha\neq 0,|\alpha|_{1}\leq d_{p}
. The following proposition is straightforward.

Proposition 6.2
.

The set
\{\chi_{\alpha}(Z)|1\leq|\alpha|_{1}\leq d_{p}\}
forms a basis for
{\mathcal{S}}
.

For the general framework, we work over this basis because as we will see in
Section

7
, the “inner kernel matrix” is convenient to state in this basis.
The
\nabla
operator also works nicely with our polynomials
\chi_{\beta}
. Indeed, observe that
\nabla_{\alpha}(\chi_{\beta})=\begin{cases}\chi_{\beta-\alpha}&\text{ if $\alpha\unlhd\beta$}\\
0&\text{ o.w.}\end{cases}
.

For a polynomial
f(Z)
in
{\mathcal{S}}
, denote by
\widehat{f}(\alpha)
the coefficient of
\chi_{\alpha}(Z)
in the expansion of
f
, that is,

f(Z)=\sum_{0\neq\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}}\widehat{f}(\alpha)\chi_{\alpha}(Z)

We can naturally extend this notation to matrices that have mean
0
. So, we can write
{\mathbf{X}}=\sum_{\alpha\neq 0}\widehat{{\mathbf{X}}}(\alpha)\chi_{\alpha}(Z)
where
\widehat{{\mathbf{X}}}(\alpha)
are deterministic matrices. In order to apply our recursion framework, we group this sum into terms based on
|\alpha|_{0}
. For
k\geq 1
, define
{\mathbf{X}}_{k}=\sum_{|\alpha|_{0}=k}\widehat{{\mathbf{X}}}(\alpha)\chi_{\alpha}(Z)
. Then,

{\mathbf{X}}=\sum_{k\geq 1}{\mathbf{X}}_{k}

Note that when
k>d_{p}
,
{\mathbf{X}}_{k}=0
.

Definition 6.3

(Indexing set
{\mathcal{K}}
)
.

We define
{\mathcal{K}}\subseteq\mathbb{N}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{n}
to be the set of pairs
(\alpha,\gamma)
such that
|\alpha|_{1}\leq d_{p},\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
and
\gamma\leq\alpha,\gamma\in\{0,1\}^{n}
.

Define the diagonal matrix
{\mathbf{D}}_{1}\in\mathbb{R}[Z]^{{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathcal{K}}}\times\mathbb{R}[Z]^{{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathcal{K}}}
with nonzero entries

{\mathbf{D}}_{1}[(I,\alpha,\gamma),(I,\alpha,\gamma)]=\sqrt{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[Z^{2\alpha\cdot(1-\gamma)}]}Z^{\alpha\cdot\gamma}

Similarly, define the diagonal matrix
{\mathbf{D}}_{2}\in\mathbb{R}[Z]^{{\mathcal{J}}\times{\mathcal{K}}}\times\mathbb{R}[Z]^{{\mathcal{J}}\times{\mathcal{K}}}
with nonzero entries

{\mathbf{D}}_{2}[(J,\alpha,\gamma),(J,\alpha,\gamma)]=\sqrt{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[Z^{2\alpha\cdot(1-\gamma)}]}Z^{\alpha\cdot\gamma}

Definition 6.4

(Matrices
{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b},{\mathbf{F}}_{k,a,b}
)
.

For integers
k,a,b
such that
k\geq 1,a,b\geq 0
, define the matrix
{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}
to have rows and columns indexed by
{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathcal{K}}
and
{\mathcal{J}}\times{\mathcal{K}}
respectively such that for all
(I,\alpha_{1},\gamma_{1})\in{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathcal{K}}
,
(J,\alpha_{2},\gamma_{2})\in{\mathcal{J}}\times{\mathcal{K}}
,

{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}[(I,\alpha_{1},\gamma_{1}),(J,\alpha_{2},\gamma_{2})]=\begin{cases}\nabla_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}{\mathbf{X}}_{k}[I,J]&\text{ if $|\alpha_{1}|_{0}=a,|\alpha_{2}|_{0}=b,\alpha_{1}\cdot\alpha_{2}=0$}\\
0&\text{o.w.}\end{cases}

Also, define
{\mathbf{F}}_{k,a,b}:={\mathbf{D}}_{1}{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}{\mathbf{D}}_{2}
.

Note that when
k>d_{p}
,
{\mathbf{F}}_{k,a,b}=0
.

Proposition 6.5
.

For integers
k,a,b
such that
k\geq 1,a,b\geq 0
, suppose
a+b<k
. Then each nonzero entry
f
of
{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}
has the property that
\widehat{f}(\alpha)
is nonzero only when
|\alpha|_{0}=k-a-b

Proof.

The nonzero entries of
{\mathbf{X}}_{k}
only has terms containing exactly
k
variables and
\nabla_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}
either zeroes out the term, or it truncates exactly
|\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}|_{0}=|\alpha_{1}|_{0}+|\alpha_{2}|_{0}=a+b
variables.

This also immediately implies that
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}]=0
whenever
a+b<k
. Finally, when
k=a+b
, we have that
{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}
is a deterministic matrix independent of the
Z_{i}
. These give rise to the matrices
{\mathbf{F}}_{a+b,a,b}
that appears in our main theorem.

We are now ready to state the main theorem.

Theorem 6.6

(General recursion)
.

Let the tuple of random variables
Z
and the function
{\mathbf{F}}
be as above. Then, for all integers
t\geq 1
,

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{F}}-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}{\mathbf{F}}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}\leq\sum_{a,b\geq 0,a+b\geq 1}(Ct^{2}dd_{p}^{4})^{(a+b)t}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{F}}_{a+b,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

for an absolute constant
C>0
.

Note that
{\mathbf{F}}_{a+b,a,b}={\mathbf{D}}_{1}{\mathbf{G}}_{a+b,a,b}{\mathbf{D}}_{2}
where
{\mathbf{D}}_{1},{\mathbf{D}}_{2}
are diagonal matrices and
{\mathbf{G}}_{a+b,a,b}
is a deterministic matrix that’s independent of
Z
. To analyze the expected Schatten norm of such matrices, we can resort to far simpler techniques. For instance, we can obtain a simple bound using an appropriate power of the Frobenius norm, and apply standard scalar concentration tools. We will see an example of this in
Section

8
.

Remark 6.7
.

We have made no attempts to optimize the factors in front of the expectation in
Theorem

6.6
, which we suspect can be improved.

We prove the main theorem by repeatedly applying the following technical lemma, the proof of which we defer to the next section.

Lemma 6.8
.

For all integers
t\geq 1
, integers
k\geq 1,a,b\geq 0
such that
a+b<k
,

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}\leq(Ct^{2}dd_{p}^{2})^{t}(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b+1}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a+1,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t})

for an absolute constant
C>0
.

Using this lemma, we can complete the proof of the main theorem.

Proof of
Theorem

6.6
.

Using
2.3
, we have
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{X}}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}\leq d_{p}^{2t}\sum_{k=1}^{d_{p}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{X}}_{k}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}
. Note that for any
k\geq 1
, the matrix
{\mathbf{X}}_{k}
is a principal submatrix of
{\mathbf{F}}_{k,0,0}
with all other entries being
0
, so
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{X}}_{k}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{F}}_{k,0,0}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}=\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,0,0}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}
. Therefore,

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{X}}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}\leq\frac{1}{2}d_{p}^{2t}\sum_{k=1}^{d_{p}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,0,0}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

We now apply
Lemma

6.8
repeatedly to all our terms until
k=a+b
, ultimately giving

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{X}}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}\leq\frac{1}{2}d_{p}^{2t}(Ct^{2}dd_{p}^{2})^{(a+b)t}\sum_{a,b\geq 0,a+b\geq 1}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{a+b,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

Observing that
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{a+b,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}=2\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{F}}_{a+b,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}
completes the proof.

7
A generalization of
[
paulin2016
]
and proof of
Lemma

6.8

In this section, we will prove
Lemma

6.8
using the high level strategy described in
Section

1
. This requires generalizing the results in
[
paulin2016
]
, and the proof techniques may be of independent interest.

1
Generalizing
[
paulin2016
]
via explicit inner kernels

In our setting, observe that
(Z,Z^{\prime})
has the same distribution as
(Z^{\prime},Z)
. This is what is known as an
exchangeable pair
of variables, that will be extremely useful for our analysis. In particular,
Z,Z^{\prime}
have the same distribution and
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}f(Z,Z^{\prime})=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}f(Z^{\prime},Z)
for every integrable function
f
.

Definition 7.1

(Laplacian operator
{\mathcal{L}}
)
.

Define the operator
{\mathcal{L}}
on the space
{\mathcal{S}}
as

{\mathcal{L}}(f)(Z)=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[f(Z)-f(Z^{\prime})|Z]

for all polynomials
f\in{\mathcal{S}}
.

Note that this operator is well-defined since for any
f\in{\mathcal{S}}
,
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[L(f)]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[f(Z)-f(Z^{\prime})|Z]]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[f(Z)-f(Z^{\prime})]=0
and hence,
L(f)\in{\mathcal{S}}
.

Lemma 7.2
.

For all
\alpha\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
,
\chi_{\alpha}
is an eigenvector of
{\mathcal{L}}
with eigenvalue
\frac{|\alpha|_{0}}{n}
.

Proof.

Recall that
Z^{\prime}
is obtained by choosing
i\in[n]
uniformly at random and then setting
Z^{\prime}=Z^{(i)}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle{\mathcal{L}}(\chi_{\alpha})(Z)

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[\chi_{\alpha}(Z)-\chi_{\alpha}(Z^{\prime})|Z]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i\leq n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[\chi_{\alpha}(Z)-\chi_{\alpha}(Z^{(i)})|Z]

When
\alpha_{i}=0
,
\chi_{\alpha}(Z)-\chi_{\alpha}(Z^{(i)})=0
. Otherwise,
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[\chi_{\alpha}(Z)-\chi_{\alpha}(Z^{(i)})|Z]=\chi_{\alpha}(Z)
. Therefore, the above expression simplifies to
\frac{|\alpha|_{0}}{n}\chi_{\alpha}(Z)
.

Theorem 7.3

(Explicit Kernel)
.

For any mean-centered polynomial
f\in{\mathcal{S}}
, there exists a polynomial
K_{f}
on
2n
variables
z_{1},\ldots,z_{n},z_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,z_{n}^{\prime}
, denoted collectively as
(z,z^{\prime})
, with the following properties

1.

K_{f}(z^{\prime},z)=-K_{f}(z,z^{\prime})

2.

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[K_{f}(Z,Z^{\prime})|Z]=f(Z)
where
(Z,Z^{\prime})
is the exchangeable pair we consider above.

Proof.

Using
6.2
and
Lemma

7.2
, under the basis of polynomials
\chi_{\alpha}
, the operator
{\mathcal{L}}
is a diagonal matrix with nonzero diagonal entries and therefore,
{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}
exists and is explicitly given by

{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}(f)(Z)=\sum_{\alpha}\frac{n}{|\alpha|_{0}}\widehat{f}(\alpha)\chi_{\alpha}(Z)

We then take
K_{f}(z,z^{\prime})={\mathcal{L}}^{-1}(f)(z)-{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}(f)(z^{\prime})
. The first condition is obvious and for the second condition, we have

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[K_{f}(Z,Z^{\prime})|Z]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}(f)(Z)-{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}(f)(Z^{\prime})|Z]={\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{L}}^{-1}(f))=f

As seen in the proof of
Theorem

7.3
,
{\mathcal{L}}
has a well-defined inverse
{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}
. We now define the matrix
{\mathbf{K}}_{k,a,b}
that we call the
inner kernel
.

Definition 7.4

(The inner kernel matrix
{\mathbf{K}}_{k,a,b}
)
.

For integers
k\geq 1,a,b\geq 0
such that
a+b<k
, define the matrix
{\mathbf{K}}_{k,a,b}\in\mathbb{R}[Z]^{{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathcal{K}}}\times\mathbb{R}[Z]^{{\mathcal{J}}\times{\mathcal{K}}}
taking
2n
variables
(z,z^{\prime})=(z_{1},\ldots,z_{n},z_{1}^{\prime},\ldots,z_{n}^{\prime})
as input as follows

{\mathbf{K}}_{k,a,b}(z,z^{\prime})={\mathcal{L}}^{-1}({\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b})(z)-{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}({\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b})(z^{\prime})

In the rest of this section except where explicitly stated, fix integers
k\geq 1,a,b\geq 0
such that
a+b<k
. Then, the inner kernel
{\mathbf{K}}_{k,a,b}
is well-defined.

Lemma 7.5
.

{\mathbf{K}}_{k,a,b}(Z,Z^{\prime})=\frac{n}{k-a-b}({\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}(Z)-{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{\prime}))

Proof.

\displaystyle{\mathbf{K}}_{k,a,b}(Z,Z^{\prime})

\displaystyle={\mathcal{L}}^{-1}({\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b})(Z)-{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}({\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b})(Z^{\prime})

\displaystyle=\sum_{|\alpha|_{0}=k-a-b}\widehat{{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}}(\alpha)({\mathcal{L}}^{-1}(\chi_{\alpha})(Z)-{\mathcal{L}}^{-1}(\chi_{\alpha})(Z^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{k-a-b}\sum_{|\alpha|_{0}=k-a-b}\widehat{{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}}(\alpha)(\chi_{\alpha}(Z)-\chi_{\alpha}(Z^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\frac{n}{k-a-b}({\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}(Z)-{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{\prime}))

The following lemma postulates important properties of the the inner kernel, including how it interacts with
{\mathbf{D}}_{1}
and
{\mathbf{D}}_{2}
.

Lemma 7.6
.

{\mathbf{K}}_{k,a,b}
satisfies the following properties

1.

{\mathbf{K}}_{k,a,b}(z^{\prime},z)=-{\mathbf{K}}_{k,a,b}(z,z^{\prime})

2.

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{K}}_{k,a,b}(Z,Z^{\prime})|Z]={\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}(Z)

3.

({\mathbf{D}}_{1}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}_{1}(Z^{\prime})){\mathbf{K}}_{k,a,b}(Z,Z^{\prime})={\mathbf{K}}_{k,a,b}(Z,Z^{\prime})({\mathbf{D}}_{2}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}_{2}(Z^{\prime}))=0
.

Proof.

The first equality is obvious from the definition. For the second equality, note that
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}]=0
and
{\mathbf{K}}_{k,a,b}
is defined by replacing each entry
f
of
{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}
by the kernel polynomial
K_{f}
as exhibited in
Theorem

7.3
. Now, we prove the third equality.

Consider the matrix
({\mathbf{D}}_{1}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}_{1}(Z^{\prime})){\mathbf{K}}_{k,a,b}(Z,Z^{\prime})
whose
[(I,\alpha_{1},\gamma_{1}),(J,\alpha_{2},\gamma_{2})]
entry is given by

\frac{n}{k-a-b}\sqrt{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[Z^{2\alpha_{1}\cdot(1-\gamma_{1})}]}(Z^{\alpha_{1}\cdot\gamma_{1}}-(Z^{\prime})^{\alpha_{1}\cdot\gamma_{1}})(\nabla_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}{\mathbf{X}}_{k}[I,J](Z)-\nabla_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}{\mathbf{X}}_{k}[I,J](Z^{\prime}))

where we have used
Lemma

7.5
.
We will argue that this term is identically
0
.
We must have
Z^{\prime}=Z^{(i)}
for some
i\leq n
. If
(\alpha_{1}\cdot\gamma_{1})_{i}=0
, then
Z^{\alpha_{1}\cdot\gamma_{1}}=(Z^{\prime})^{\alpha_{1}\cdot\gamma_{1}}
and the above term is
0
.
Otherwise,
(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2})_{i}\neq 0
and so
\nabla_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}
on any polynomial
f
will only contain the terms independent of
Z_{i}
, in which case
\nabla_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}{\mathbf{X}}_{k}[I,J](Z)=\nabla_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}}{\mathbf{X}}_{k}[I,J](Z^{\prime})
. In this case was well, the above term is
0
. The proof of the other equality is analogous.

The reason we call
{\mathbf{K}}_{k,a,b}
the inner kernel is because, as seen above, it serves as a kernel for the inner matrix
{\mathbf{G}}
in the decomposition
{\mathbf{F}}={\mathbf{D}}{\mathbf{G}}{\mathbf{D}}
.

Since we will need to work with Hermitian dilations, we define

{\mathbf{D}}=\begin{bmatrix}{\mathbf{D}}_{1}&0\\
0&{\mathbf{D}}_{2}\end{bmatrix}

We will use the following basic fact extensively in our manipulations.

Fact 7.7
.

For any matrix
{\mathbf{A}}\in\mathbb{R}[Z]^{{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathcal{K}}}\times\mathbb{R}[Z]^{{\mathcal{J}}\times{\mathcal{K}}}
,
{\mathbf{D}}\overline{{\mathbf{A}}}{\mathbf{D}}=\overline{{\mathbf{D}}_{1}{\mathbf{A}}{\mathbf{D}}_{2}}
.

Proof.

We have

\displaystyle{\mathbf{D}}\overline{{\mathbf{A}}}{\mathbf{D}}=\begin{bmatrix}{\mathbf{D}}_{1}&0\\
0&{\mathbf{D}}_{2}\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}0&{\mathbf{A}}\\
{\mathbf{A}}^{\intercal}&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathbf{D}}_{1}&0\\
0&{\mathbf{D}}_{2}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}0&{\mathbf{D}}_{1}{\mathbf{A}}\\
{\mathbf{D}}_{2}{\mathbf{A}}^{\intercal}&0\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}{\mathbf{D}}_{1}&0\\
0&{\mathbf{D}}_{2}\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}0&{\mathbf{D}}_{1}{\mathbf{A}}{\mathbf{D}}_{2}\\
{\mathbf{D}}_{2}{\mathbf{A}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}_{1}&0\end{bmatrix}

\displaystyle=\overline{{\mathbf{D}}_{1}{\mathbf{A}}{\mathbf{D}}_{2}}

We start with a generalized version of a result from
[
paulin2016
]
.

Lemma 7.8
.

Let
{\mathbf{K}}=\overline{{\mathbf{K}}}_{k,a,b}
. For any symmetric matrix valued function
{\mathbf{R}}
on the variables
Z
of the same dimensions as
{\mathbf{K}}
, such that
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{K}}(Z,Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{R}}(Z)\right\rVert<\infty
, we have

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}(Z){\mathbf{R}}(Z)]=\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{D}}(Z){\mathbf{K}}(Z,Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{D}}(Z)({\mathbf{R}}(Z)-{\mathbf{R}}(Z^{\prime}))]

Proof.

By
Lemma

7.6
, we have

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}(Z){\mathbf{R}}(Z)]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{D}}(Z)\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z){\mathbf{D}}(Z){\mathbf{R}}(Z)]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{D}}(Z)\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{K}}(Z,Z^{\prime})|Z]{\mathbf{D}}(Z){\mathbf{R}}(Z)]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{D}}(Z){\mathbf{K}}(Z,Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{D}}(Z){\mathbf{R}}(Z)]

where the first equality follow from condition
2
of
Lemma

7.6
and the second follows from the pull-through property of expectations. Continuing,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}(Z){\mathbf{R}}(Z)]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{D}}(Z){\mathbf{K}}(Z,Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{D}}(Z){\mathbf{R}}(Z)]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{K}}(Z^{\prime},Z){\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{R}}(Z^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{K}}(Z,Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{R}}(Z^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{D}}(Z){\mathbf{K}}(Z,Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{R}}(Z^{\prime})]

\displaystyle=-\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{D}}(Z){\mathbf{K}}(Z,Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{D}}(Z){\mathbf{R}}(Z^{\prime})]

Here, the second equality follows from the fact that
(Z,Z^{\prime})
has the same distribution as
(Z^{\prime},Z)
, so we can exchange them. The third, fourth and fifth equalities follow from conditions
1,3,3
of
Lemma

7.6
respectively. Adding the two displays, we get the result.

Definition 7.9

(Matrices
{\mathbf{U}}_{k,a,b},{\mathbf{V}}_{k,a,b}
)
.

We define the following matrices

{\mathbf{U}}_{k,a,b}=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[(\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}(Z)-\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{\prime}))^{2}|Z]

{\mathbf{V}}_{k,a,b}=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[({\mathbf{D}}(Z)\overline{{\mathbf{K}}}_{k,a,b}(Z,Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{D}}(Z))^{2}|Z]

The definition of
{\mathbf{U}}_{k,a,b}
is essentially unchanged from
[
paulin2016
]
, where it is called the
conditional variance
. The definition of
{\mathbf{V}}_{k,a,b}
is slightly different in our setting. This lets us exploit the specific product structure exhibited by
\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}
and the special properties of the inner kernel from
Lemma

7.6
.

We will now prove a lemma which is similar to a lemma shown in
[
paulin2016
]
.

Lemma 7.10
.

For any
s>0
and for any integer
t\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{2t-1}{4}\right)^{t}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert s{\mathbf{U}}_{k,a,b}+s^{-1}{\mathbf{V}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}

To prove this, we will need the following inequality.

Lemma 7.11

(Polynomial mean value trace inequality,
[
paulin2016
]
)
.

For all matrices
{\mathbf{A}},{\mathbf{B}},{\mathbf{C}}\in\mathbb{H}^{d}
, all integers
q\geq 1
and all
s>0
,

\displaystyle\tr[{\mathbf{C}}({\mathbf{A}}^{q}-{\mathbf{B}}^{q})]|\leq\frac{q}{4}\tr[(s({\mathbf{A}}-{\mathbf{B}})^{2}+s^{-1}{\mathbf{C}}^{2})({\mathbf{A}}^{q-1}+{\mathbf{B}}^{q-1})]

Proof of
Lemma

7.10
.

We start by invoking
Lemma

7.8
by setting
{\mathbf{R}}(Z)=\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}^{2t-1}(Z)
.

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\tr[\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}\cdot\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}^{2t-1}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{D}}(Z)\overline{{\mathbf{K}}}_{k,a,b}(Z,Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{D}}(Z)(\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}^{2t-1}(Z)-\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}^{2t-1}(Z^{\prime}))]

Applying
Lemma

7.11
,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

\displaystyle\leq(\frac{2t-1}{8})\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\tr[(s(\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}(Z)-\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{\prime}))^{2}+s^{-1}({\mathbf{D}}(Z)\overline{{\mathbf{K}}}_{k,a,b}(Z,Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{D}}(Z))^{2})(\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}^{2t-2}(Z)+\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}^{2t-2}(Z^{\prime}))]

\displaystyle=(\frac{2t-1}{4})\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\tr[(s(\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}(Z)-\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{\prime}))^{2}+s^{-1}({\mathbf{D}}(Z)\overline{{\mathbf{K}}}_{k,a,b}(Z,Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{D}}(Z))^{2})\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}^{2t-2}(Z)]

where the last line used the fact that
(Z,Z^{\prime})
has the same distribution as
(Z^{\prime},Z)
and applied condition
3
of
Lemma

7.6
. Using the definitions of
{\mathbf{U}}_{k,a,b}
and
{\mathbf{V}}_{k,a,b}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2t-1}{4}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\tr[(s{\mathbf{U}}_{k,a,b}+s^{-1}{\mathbf{V}}_{k,a,b})\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}^{2t-2}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2t-1}{4}\left(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert s{\mathbf{U}}_{k,a,b}+s^{-1}{\mathbf{V}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}\right)^{1/t}(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t})^{(t-1)/t}

where we used Hölder’s inequality for the trace and Hölder’s inequality for the expectation. Rearranging gives the result.

2
Proof of
Lemma

6.8

Lemma

7.10
suggests that in order to bound
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}
, it suffices to bound
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{U}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}
and
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{V}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}
. Indeed, this will be our strategy. To bound
\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{U}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}
, we will bound it via the matrices that we define below.

Definition 7.12

(Matrices
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{1}^{k,a,b},{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{2}^{k,a,b},{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{3}^{k,a,b}
)
.

Define the matrices

{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{1}^{k,a,b}=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[(({\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime}))\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z){\mathbf{D}}(Z))^{2}|Z]

{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{2}^{k,a,b}=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[({\mathbf{D}}(Z)(\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z)-\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{\prime})){\mathbf{D}}(Z))^{2}|Z]

{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{3}^{k,a,b}=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[({\mathbf{D}}(Z)\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z)({\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime})))^{2}|Z]

Lemma 7.13
.

{\mathbf{U}}_{k,a,b}\preceq 3({\mathbf{\Delta}}_{1}^{k,a,b}+{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{2}^{k,a,b}+{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{3}^{k,a,b})
.

To prove this lemma, we will use the following lemma.

Lemma 7.14
.

We have the relations

({\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime}))(\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z){\mathbf{D}}(Z)-\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime}))=0

(\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z)-\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{\prime}))({\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime}))=0

Proof sketch.

The proof is similar to the proof of third equality in
Lemma

7.6
. When
Z^{\prime}
is set to
Z^{(i)}
for some
i\leq n
, when a diagonal entry of
{\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime})
is nonzero, then the corresponding row of
\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z){\mathbf{D}}(Z)-\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime})
will be
0
. The second equality is analogous.

Proof of
Lemma

7.13
.

We have

\displaystyle(\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}(Z)-\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{\prime}))^{2}

\displaystyle=({\mathbf{D}}(Z)\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z){\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime})\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime}))^{2}

\displaystyle=\bigg({\mathbf{D}}(Z)\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z)({\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime}))+{\mathbf{D}}(Z)(\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z)-\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{\prime})){\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime})

\displaystyle\qquad+({\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime}))\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime})\bigg)^{2}

\displaystyle=\bigg({\mathbf{D}}(Z)\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z)({\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime}))+{\mathbf{D}}(Z)(\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z)-\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{\prime})){\mathbf{D}}(Z)

\displaystyle\qquad+({\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime}))\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z){\mathbf{D}}(Z)\bigg)^{2}

where the last equality follows from
Lemma

7.14
. Taking expectations conditioned on
Z
and applying
2.2
, we immediately get
{\mathbf{U}}_{k,a,b}\preceq 3({\mathbf{\Delta}}_{1}^{k,a,b}+{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{2}^{k,a,b}+{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{3}^{k,a,b})
.

In subsequent sections, we will prove the following technical bounds on the matrices we have considered so far.

Lemma 7.15
.

For all integers
t\geq 1
,

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{2}^{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}\leq\frac{(2d_{p})^{t}}{n^{t}}(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b+1}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a+1,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t})

Lemma 7.16
.

For all integers
t\geq 1
,

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{V}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}\leq(2d_{p})^{t}n^{t}(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b+1}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a+1,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t})

Lemma 7.17
.

For all integers
t\geq 1
,

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{1}^{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}\leq\frac{(8dd_{p})^{t}}{n^{t}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

Lemma 7.18
.

For all integers
t\geq 1
,

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{3}^{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}\leq\frac{(4d_{p})^{t}}{n^{t}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

Assuming the above lemmas, we can complete the proof of
Lemma

6.8
, which we restate for convenience.

See
6.8

Proof of
Lemma

6.8
.

Using
Lemma

7.10
,
Lemma

7.13
, we get that for any
s>0
,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

\displaystyle\leq(\frac{2t-1}{4})^{t}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert s{\mathbf{U}}_{k,a,b}+s^{-1}{\mathbf{V}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}

\displaystyle\leq t^{t}(s^{t}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{U}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}+s^{-t}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{V}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t})

\displaystyle\leq(9st)^{t}(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{1}^{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{2}^{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{3}^{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t})+t^{t}s^{-t}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{V}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}

Let
\rho=s/n
. Since the inequality is true for any choice of
s>0
, it is true for any choice of
\rho>0
.
Now, using
Lemma

7.17
,
Lemma

7.18
,

\displaystyle(9st)^{t}(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{1}^{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{3}^{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t})

\displaystyle\leq(9st)^{t}\bigg(\frac{(8dd_{p})^{t}}{n^{t}}+\frac{(4d_{p})^{t}}{n^{t}}\bigg)\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

\displaystyle=\rho^{t}(C_{1}tdd_{p})^{t}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

for an absolute constant
C_{1}>0
. Using
Lemma

7.15
,
Lemma

7.16
,

\displaystyle(9st)^{t}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{2}^{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}+t^{t}s^{-t}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{V}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}

\displaystyle\leq\bigg((9st)^{t}\frac{(2d_{p})^{t}}{n^{t}}+t^{t}s^{-t}(2d_{p})^{t}n^{t}\bigg)(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b+1}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a+1,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t})

\displaystyle\leq(\rho^{t}C_{2}^{t}+\rho^{-t}C_{3}^{t})(td_{p})^{t}(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b+1}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a+1,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t})

for absolute constants
C_{2},C_{3}>0
.
Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

\displaystyle\leq\rho^{t}(C_{1}tdd_{p})^{t}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

\displaystyle\qquad+(\rho^{t}C_{2}^{t}+\rho^{-t}C_{3}^{t})(td_{p})^{t}(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b+1}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a+1,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t})

We choose
\rho>0
so that
\rho^{t}(C_{1}tdd_{p})^{t}=\frac{1}{2}
to get

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+\frac{1}{2}(Ct^{2}dd_{p}^{2})^{t}(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b+1}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a+1,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t})

for an absolute constant
C>0
.
Rearranging yields the result.

3
Bounding
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{2}^{k,a,b}
and
{\mathbf{V}}_{k,a,b}

The next lemma relates
{\mathbf{V}}_{k,a,b}
to
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{2}^{k,a,b}
upto a factor of
n^{2}
which will be enough for us. We can then focus on bounding
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{2}^{k,a,b}
.

Lemma 7.19
.

{\mathbf{V}}_{k,a,b}\preceq n^{2}{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{2}^{k,a,b}

Proof.

Using
Lemma

7.5
,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{V}}_{k,a,b}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[({\mathbf{D}}(Z)\overline{{\mathbf{K}}}_{k,a,b}(Z,Z^{\prime}){\mathbf{D}}(Z))^{2}|Z]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[({\mathbf{D}}(Z)\bigg(\frac{n}{k-a-b}(\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z)-\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{\prime}))\bigg){\mathbf{D}}(Z))^{2}|Z]

\displaystyle\preceq n^{2}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[({\mathbf{D}}(Z)(\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z)-\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{\prime})){\mathbf{D}}(Z))^{2}|Z]

\displaystyle=n^{2}{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{2}^{k,a,b}

For
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq l\leq d
, let
\mathbf{e}_{i,l}\in\mathbb{N}^{n}
denote the vector
\alpha
with
\alpha_{i}=l
and
\alpha_{j}=0
for
j\neq i
.
We note the following simple proposition.

Proposition 7.20
.

For any polynomial
f
such that the degree of
Z_{i}
is at most
d
,

f(Z)-f(Z^{(i)})=\sum_{1\leq l\leq d}(Z_{i}^{l}-\widetilde{Z_{i}}^{l})\nabla_{\mathbf{e}_{i,l}}(f)

We now restate and prove
Lemma

7.15
.

See
7.15

Proof.

Consider

\displaystyle{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{2}^{k,a,b}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[({\mathbf{D}}(Z)(\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z)-\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{\prime})){\mathbf{D}}(Z))^{2}|Z]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\bigg[\begin{bmatrix}{\mathbf{M}}{\mathbf{M}}^{\intercal}&0\\
0&{\mathbf{M}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{M}}\end{bmatrix}|Z\bigg]

\displaystyle=\begin{bmatrix}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{M}}{\mathbf{M}}^{\intercal}|Z]&0\\
0&\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{M}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{M}}|Z]\end{bmatrix}

where
{\mathbf{M}}={\mathbf{D}}_{1}(Z)({\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}(Z)-{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{\prime})){\mathbf{D}}_{2}(Z)
. Using
7.20
,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{M}}{\mathbf{M}}^{T}|Z]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{D}}_{1}(Z)({\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}(Z)-{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{\prime})){\mathbf{D}}_{2}(Z)\cdot{\mathbf{D}}_{2}(Z)({\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}(Z)-{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{\prime}))^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}_{1}(Z)|Z]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{D}}_{1}(Z)({\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}(Z)-{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{(i)})){\mathbf{D}}_{2}(Z)\cdot{\mathbf{D}}_{2}(Z)({\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}(Z)-{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b}(Z^{(i)}))^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}_{1}(Z)|Z]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{l=1}^{d}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[(Z_{i}^{l}-\widetilde{Z_{i}}^{l})^{2}|Z]\cdot{\mathbf{D}}_{1}(Z)(\nabla_{\mathbf{e}_{i,l}}{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b})(Z){\mathbf{D}}_{2}(Z)\cdot{\mathbf{D}}_{2}(Z)(\nabla_{\mathbf{e}_{i,l}}{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b})(Z)^{\intercal}{\mathbf{D}}_{1}(Z)

Define
{\mathbf{N}}_{i,l}(Z):={\mathbf{D}}_{1}(Z)(\nabla_{\mathbf{e}_{i,l}}{\mathbf{G}}_{k,a,b})(Z){\mathbf{D}}_{2}(Z)
. Then,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{M}}{\mathbf{M}}^{T}|Z]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{l=1}^{d}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[(Z_{i}^{l}-\widetilde{Z_{i}}^{l})^{2}|Z]\cdot{\mathbf{N}}_{i,l}(Z){\mathbf{N}}_{i,l}(Z)^{\intercal}

\displaystyle\preceq\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{l=1}^{d}(Z_{i}^{2l}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{i}^{2l}])\cdot{\mathbf{N}}_{i,l}(Z){\mathbf{N}}_{i,l}(Z)^{\intercal}

Similarly,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{M}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{M}}|Z]

\displaystyle\preceq\frac{2}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{l=1}^{d}(Z_{i}^{2l}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{i}^{2l}])\cdot{\mathbf{N}}_{i,l}(Z)^{\intercal}{\mathbf{N}}_{i,l}(Z)

Claim 7.21
.

We have the relations

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{l=1}^{d}(Z_{i}^{2l}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{i}^{2l}])\cdot{\mathbf{N}}_{i,l}(Z){\mathbf{N}}_{i,l}(Z)^{\intercal}=(b+1){\mathbf{F}}_{k,a,b+1}{\mathbf{F}}_{k,a,b+1}^{\intercal}

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{l=1}^{d}(Z_{i}^{2l}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{i}^{2l}])\cdot{\mathbf{N}}_{i,l}(Z)^{\intercal}{\mathbf{N}}_{i,l}(Z)=(a+1){\mathbf{F}}_{k,a+1,b}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{F}}_{k,a+1,b}

Using this claim, we have

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{M}}{\mathbf{M}}^{T}|Z]\preceq\frac{2(b+1)}{n}{\mathbf{F}}_{k,a,b+1}{\mathbf{F}}_{k,a,b+1}^{\intercal}\preceq\frac{2d_{p}}{n}{\mathbf{F}}_{k,a,b+1}{\mathbf{F}}_{k,a,b+1}^{\intercal}

\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{M}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{M}}|Z]\preceq\frac{2(a+1)}{n}{\mathbf{F}}_{k,a+1,b}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{F}}_{k,a+1,b}\preceq\frac{2d_{p}}{n}{\mathbf{F}}_{k,a+1,b}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{F}}_{k,a+1,b}

Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{2}^{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{M}}{\mathbf{M}}^{\intercal}|Z]\right\rVert_{t}^{t}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[{\mathbf{M}}^{\intercal}{\mathbf{M}}|Z]\right\rVert_{t}^{t}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(2d_{p})^{t}}{n^{t}}(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{F}}_{k,a,b+1}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{F}}_{k,a+1,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{(2d_{p})^{t}}{n^{t}}(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b+1}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a+1,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t})

It remains to prove the claim.

Proof of
7.21
.

We will prove the first relation, the second is analogous.
For a fixed
i\leq n,l\leq d
, consider any nonzero entry
[(I_{1},\alpha_{1},\gamma_{1}),(I_{2},\alpha_{2},\gamma_{2})]
of
\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sum_{l=1}^{d}(Z_{i}^{2l}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{i}^{2l}]){\mathbf{N}}_{i,l}(Z){\mathbf{N}}_{i,l}(Z)^{\intercal}
, where
I_{1},I_{2}\in{\mathcal{I}},(\alpha_{1},\gamma_{1}),(\alpha_{2},\gamma_{2})\in{\mathcal{K}}
. We must have
|\alpha_{1}|_{0}=|\alpha_{2}|_{0}=a
, in which case the entry is equal to

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(J,\alpha_{3},\gamma_{3})\in{\mathcal{J}}\times{\mathcal{K}}\\
|\alpha_{3}|=b\\
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}=0\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle(Z_{i}^{2l}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[Z_{i}^{2l}])\cdot(\sqrt{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[Z^{2\alpha_{1}\cdot(1-\gamma_{1})+2\alpha_{3}\cdot(1-\gamma_{3})}]}Z^{\alpha_{1}\cdot\gamma_{1}+\alpha_{3}\cdot\gamma_{3}}\nabla_{\mathbf{e}_{i,l}}\nabla_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{3}}{\mathbf{X}}_{k}[I_{1},J])

\displaystyle\cdot(\sqrt{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[Z^{2\alpha_{2}\cdot(1-\gamma_{2})+2\alpha_{3}\cdot(1-\gamma_{3})}]}Z^{\alpha_{2}\cdot\gamma_{2}+\alpha_{3}\cdot\gamma_{3}}\nabla_{\mathbf{e}_{i,l}}\nabla_{\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}}{\mathbf{X}}_{k}[I_{2},J])

Note that the term inside the summation is nonzero only when
\mathbf{e}_{i,l}\cdot(\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{3})=\mathbf{e}_{i,l}\cdot(\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3})=0
. Hence, this sum can be written as

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(J,\alpha_{3},\gamma_{3})\in{\mathcal{J}}\times{\mathcal{K}}\\
|\alpha_{3}|=b+1\\
\mathbf{e}_{i,l}\unlhd\alpha_{3},\alpha_{1}\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}=0\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle(\sqrt{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[Z^{2\alpha_{1}\cdot(1-\gamma_{1})+2\alpha_{3}\cdot(1-\gamma_{3})}]}Z^{\alpha_{1}\cdot\gamma_{1}+\alpha_{3}\cdot\gamma_{3}}\nabla_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{3}}{\mathbf{X}}_{k}[I_{1},J])

\displaystyle\cdot(\sqrt{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[Z^{2\alpha_{2}\cdot(1-\gamma_{2})+2\alpha_{3}\cdot(1-\gamma_{3})}]}Z^{\alpha_{2}\cdot\gamma_{2}+\alpha_{3}\cdot\gamma_{3}}\nabla_{\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}}{\mathbf{X}}_{k}[I_{2},J])

When we add this entry over all
i\leq n,l\leq d
, this simplifies to

\displaystyle(b+1)\cdot\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}(J,\alpha_{3},\gamma_{3})\in{\mathcal{J}}\times{\mathcal{K}}\\
|\alpha_{3}|=b+1\\
\alpha_{1}\alpha_{3}=\alpha_{2}\alpha_{3}=0\end{subarray}}

\displaystyle(\sqrt{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[Z^{2\alpha_{1}\cdot(1-\gamma_{1})+2\alpha_{3}\cdot(1-\gamma_{3})}]}Z^{\alpha_{1}\cdot\gamma_{1}+\alpha_{3}\cdot\gamma_{3}}\nabla_{\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{3}}{\mathbf{X}}_{k}[I_{1},J])

\displaystyle\cdot(\sqrt{\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[Z^{2\alpha_{2}\cdot(1-\gamma_{2})+2\alpha_{3}\cdot(1-\gamma_{3})}]}Z^{\alpha_{2}\cdot\gamma_{2}+\alpha_{3}\cdot\gamma_{3}}\nabla_{\alpha_{2}+\alpha_{3}}{\mathbf{X}}_{k}[I_{2},J])

The factor of
(b+1)
came because the index
i
could have been chosen from among all the active indices in
\alpha_{3}
. But this is precisely the
[(I_{1},\alpha_{1},\gamma_{1}),(I_{2},\alpha_{2},\gamma_{2})]
entry of
(b+1){\mathbf{F}}_{k,a,b+1}{\mathbf{F}}_{k,a,b+1}^{\intercal}
, proving the claim.

We restate and prove
Lemma

7.16
.

See
7.16

Proof.

Using
Lemma

7.19
and
Lemma

7.15
, we get

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{V}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}

\displaystyle\leq n^{2t}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{2}^{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}

\displaystyle\leq(2d_{p})^{t}n^{t}(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b+1}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a+1,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t})

4
Bounding
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{1}^{k,a,b}
and
{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{3}^{k,a,b}

Define
\sqcup
to be the disjoint union of sets. For
1\leq i\leq n
and
1\leq l\leq d
, define the diagonal matrices
{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i,l},{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i,l}^{\prime},{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i},{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{({\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathcal{K}})\sqcup({\mathcal{J}}\times{\mathcal{K}})}\times\mathbb{R}^{({\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathcal{K}})\sqcup({\mathcal{J}}\times{\mathcal{K}})}
(the same dimensions as
{\mathbf{D}}
) as

{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i,l}[(I,\alpha,\beta),(I,\alpha,\beta)]=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if $(\alpha\cdot\gamma)_{i}\neq 0$ and $\alpha_{i}=l$}\\
0&\text{ o.w.}\end{cases}\hskip 17.00024pt{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}[(I,\alpha,\beta),(I,\alpha,\beta)]=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if $(\alpha\cdot\gamma)_{i}\neq 0$}\\
0&\text{ o.w.}\end{cases}

{\mathbf{\Pi}}^{\prime}_{i,l}[(I,\alpha,\beta),(I,\alpha,\beta)]=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if $\alpha_{i}\neq 0$ and $\alpha_{i}=l$}\\
0&\text{ o.w.}\end{cases}\hskip 17.00024pt{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}^{\prime}[(I,\alpha,\beta),(I,\alpha,\beta)]=\begin{cases}1&\text{ if $\alpha_{i}\neq 0$}\\
0&\text{ o.w.}\end{cases}

for all
I\in{\mathcal{I}}\sqcup{\mathcal{J}}
.
Note that for all
i\leq n
,
{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}=\sum_{l=1}^{d}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i,l}
.

Also, for all
1\leq i\leq n
, we define the permutation matrices
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{({\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathcal{K}})\sqcup({\mathcal{J}}\times{\mathcal{K}})}\times\mathbb{R}^{({\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathcal{K}})\sqcup({\mathcal{J}}\times{\mathcal{K}})}
as follows. Consider the permutation
\sigma_{1}
on
{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathcal{K}}
that transposes
(I,\alpha,\gamma)
and
(I,\alpha,\gamma+\mathbf{e}_{i})
for all
(I,\alpha,\gamma)\in{\mathcal{I}}\times{\mathcal{K}}
such that
\alpha_{i}\neq 0
. Here,
\mathbf{e}_{i}\in\{0,1\}^{n}
has exactly one nonzero entry, which is in the
i
th position, and
\gamma+\mathbf{e}_{i}
is the usual addition over
\mathbb{F}_{2}
.
\sigma_{1}
leaves other positions fixed. Let
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}^{(1)}_{i}
be the permutation matrix for
\sigma
. Similarly, let
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}^{(2)}_{i}
be the permutation matrix of the permutation
\sigma_{2}
on
{\mathcal{J}}\times{\mathcal{K}}
that transposes
(J,\alpha,\gamma)
and
(J,\alpha,\gamma+\mathbf{e}_{i})
for all
(J,\alpha,\gamma)\in{\mathcal{J}}\times{\mathcal{K}}
such that
\alpha_{i}\neq 0
, and leaves all other positions fixed. Then, we define
{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}=\begin{bmatrix}{\mathbf{\Sigma}}^{(1)}_{i}&0\\
0&{\mathbf{\Sigma}}^{(2)}_{i}\end{bmatrix}
. The following fact is easy to verify.

Fact 7.22
.

{\mathbf{\Pi}}^{\prime}_{i,l}{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}={\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}{\mathbf{\Pi}}^{\prime}_{i,l}
and
{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}^{\prime}{\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}={\mathbf{\Sigma}}_{i}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}^{\prime}
.

We are now ready to prove
Lemma

7.17
which we restate for convenience.

See
7.17

Proof.

Firstly,

\displaystyle{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{1}^{k,a,b}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[(({\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime}))\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z){\mathbf{D}}(Z))^{2}|Z]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[({\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime}))\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z){\mathbf{D}}(Z)\cdot{\mathbf{D}}(Z)\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z)({\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime}))|Z]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[({\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime})){\mathbf{M}}(Z)({\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{\prime}))|Z]

where we define
{\mathbf{M}}(Z)=\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z){\mathbf{D}}(Z)\cdot{\mathbf{D}}(Z)\overline{{\mathbf{G}}}_{k,a,b}(Z)
.
Recall that
Z^{\prime}=Z^{(i)}
for some
i
randomly chosen from
[n]
uniformly. Observing that
{\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{(i)})={\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}({\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{(i)}))
for all
i
, we get

\displaystyle{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{1}^{k,a,b}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[n]}[({\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{(i)})){\mathbf{M}}(Z)({\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{(i)}))]|Z]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[n]}[{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}({\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{(i)})){\mathbf{M}}(Z)({\mathbf{D}}(Z)-{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{(i)})){\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}]|Z]

\displaystyle\preceq 2\bigg(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[n]}[{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}{\mathbf{D}}(Z){\mathbf{M}}(Z){\mathbf{D}}(Z){\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}]|Z]+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[n]}[{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{(i)}){\mathbf{M}}(Z){\mathbf{D}}(Z^{(i)}){\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}]|Z]\bigg)

\displaystyle\preceq 2\bigg(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[n]}[{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}^{2}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}]+\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[n]}[{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{(i)}){\mathbf{M}}(Z){\mathbf{D}}(Z^{(i)}){\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}]|Z]\bigg)

\displaystyle\preceq 2({\mathbf{\Delta}}_{10}+{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{11})

where we define

{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{10}=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[n]}[{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}^{2}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}],\qquad{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{11}=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[n]}[{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{(i)}){\mathbf{M}}(Z){\mathbf{D}}(Z^{(i)}){\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}]|Z]

Invoking
Lemma

2.4
over the interval
[0,\infty)
with the convex continuous function
f(x)=x^{t}
,
{\mathbf{B}}_{i}=\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}^{2},{\mathbf{A}}_{i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{p}}}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}
where we observe that
\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbf{A}}_{i}{\mathbf{A}}_{i}^{T}=\frac{1}{d_{p}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}^{2}\preceq{\mathbf{I}}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{10}\right\rVert_{t}^{t}=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\tr[{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{10}^{t}]=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\tr[\bigg(\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[n]}[{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}^{2}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}]\bigg)^{t}]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{n^{t}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\tr[\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}^{2}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}\bigg)^{t}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{d_{p}^{t-1}}{n^{t}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\tr[\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}^{2t}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}\bigg)]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{d_{p}^{t-1}}{n^{t}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\tr[\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}^{2}\bigg)\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}^{2t}]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{d_{p}^{t}}{n^{t}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\tr[\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}^{2t}]

\displaystyle=\frac{d_{p}^{t}}{n^{t}}\mathop{\mathbb{E}}\left\lVert\overline{{\mathbf{F}}}_{k,a,b}\right\rVert_{2t}^{2t}

Now, consider

\displaystyle{\mathbf{\Delta}}_{11}

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[n]}[{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{(i)}){\mathbf{M}}(Z){\mathbf{D}}(Z^{(i)}){\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i}]|Z]

\displaystyle=\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[n]}[(\sum_{l=1}^{d}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i,l}){\mathbf{D}}(Z^{(i)}){\mathbf{M}}(Z){\mathbf{D}}(Z^{(i)})(\sum_{l=1}^{d}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i,l})]|Z]

\displaystyle\preceq d\cdot\mathop{\mathbb{E}}[\mathop{\mathbb{E}}_{i\in[n]}[\sum_{l=1}^{d}{\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i,l}{\mathbf{D}}(Z^{(i)}){\mathbf{M}}(Z){\mathbf{D}}(Z^{(i)}){\mathbf{\Pi}}_{i,l}]|Z]
