}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.7803pt}{-2.64435pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})
. Modify the label of
\{\scalebox{0.85}{\footnotesize\hbox to9.68pt{\vbox to9.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.83905pt\lower-4.83905pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.42 6.42 L -6.42 6.42 L -6.42 -6.42 L 6.42 -6.42 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.7803pt}{-2.64435pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}},\scalebox{0.85}{\footnotesize\hbox to12.78pt{\vbox to12.78pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.38823pt\lower-6.38823pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.56 0 C 8.56 4.73 4.73 8.56 0 8.56 C -4.73 8.56 -8.56 4.73 -8.56 0 C -8.56 -4.73 -4.73 -8.56 0 -8.56 C 4.73 -8.56 8.56 -4.73 8.56 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.794pt}{-1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\}
to any number up to
\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert
.

•

Pick a square vertex
\scalebox{0.85}{\footnotesize\hbox to11.49pt{\vbox to11.49pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.7467pt\lower-5.7467pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.67 7.67 L -7.67 7.67 L -7.67 -7.67 L 7.67 -7.67 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.28262pt}{-1.86658pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\in V(\gamma)
to be the second end vertex. Optionally uncollapse it by adding a new square to
\gamma
in the same way as above.

The process can be carried out in at most
\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert^{3}\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert(\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert+1)\leq 2\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert^{3}\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert^{2}
ways. We multiply by 2 to accommodate right spiders.

Let us label each parent-child edge
(\gamma,\beta
) as either a “type 1” edge if
\beta\in{\mathcal{I}}_{\gamma}^{(1)}
or a “type 2” edge if
\beta\in{\mathcal{I}}_{\gamma}^{(2)}
.

Proposition 4.39
.

Let
p
be a path in
W(\alpha)
with
\#_{1}(p)
type 1 edges and
\#_{2}(p)
type 2 edges. Then
\#_{1}(p)~\leq~\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert~+~2\#_{2}(p)
.

Proof.

For a shape
\gamma
, let
S_{\gamma}
be the set of square vertices in
\gamma
. Then,
S_{\gamma}\cap W_{\gamma}
will be the set of middle vertices of
\gamma
which are squares.
We claim that the quantity
\left\lvert{\mathcal{S}}_{\gamma}\cap W_{\gamma}\right\rvert+\left\lvert U_{\gamma}\setminus(U_{\gamma}\cap V_{\gamma})\right\rvert+\left\lvert V_{\gamma}\setminus(U_{\gamma}\cap V_{\gamma})\right\rvert
decreases during a collapse.

Fix a pair of consecutive shapes
(\gamma,\beta)
which form a type
1 edge. Looking at the definition of
{\mathcal{I}}_{\gamma}^{(1)}
, each end vertex either
collapses with (1) nothing, or (2) a vertex of
W_{\gamma}
, or (3) a vertex from

V_{\gamma}\setminus U_{\gamma}
(if
\gamma
is a left spider;
for a right spider,
U_{\gamma}\setminus V_{\gamma}
).
Furthermore, case (2) or (3) must occur for at least one of the end vertices and also, they do not collapse together.

If case (2) occurs, then
\left\lvert{\mathcal{S}}_{\beta}\cap W_{\beta}\right\rvert<\left\lvert{\mathcal{S}}_{\gamma}\cap W_{\gamma}\right\rvert
while
\left\lvert U_{\beta}\setminus(U_{\beta}\cap V_{\beta})\right\rvert=\left\lvert U_{\gamma}\setminus(U_{\gamma}\cap V_{\gamma})\right\rvert
and
\left\lvert V_{\beta}\setminus(U_{\beta}\cap V_{\beta})\right\rvert=\left\lvert V_{\gamma}\setminus(U_{\gamma}\cap V_{\gamma})\right\rvert
.
On the other hand, if case (3) occurs, then
W_{\beta}=W_{\gamma}
while

\left\lvert U_{\beta}\setminus(U_{\beta}\cap V_{\beta})\right\rvert<\left\lvert U_{\gamma}\setminus(U_{\gamma}\cap V_{\gamma})\right\rvert
and
\left\lvert V_{\beta}\setminus(U_{\beta}\cap V_{\beta})\right\rvert<\left\lvert V_{\gamma}\setminus(U_{\gamma}\cap V_{\gamma})\right\rvert
.
In all cases,
\left\lvert{\mathcal{S}}_{\beta}\cap W_{\beta}\right\rvert+\left\lvert U_{\beta}\setminus(U_{\beta}\cap V_{\beta})\right\rvert+\left\lvert V_{\beta}\setminus(U_{\beta}\cap V_{\beta})\right\rvert<\left\lvert{\mathcal{S}}_{\gamma}\cap W_{\gamma}\right\rvert+\left\lvert U_{\gamma}\setminus(U_{\gamma}\cap V_{\gamma})\right\rvert+\left\lvert V_{\gamma}\setminus(U_{\gamma}\cap V_{\gamma})\right\rvert
as desired.

Now we bound this expression for
\alpha
. From the definition of
{\mathcal{L}}
,
Definition

3.6
,
for spiders appearing in the pseudocalibration,
the square vertices in
W_{\alpha}
,
U_{\alpha}\setminus(U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha})
and
V_{\alpha}\setminus(U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha})
have degree at least
1
and can only be connected to circle vertices.
Therefore their number is bounded by
\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert
. Hence, initially

\left\lvert{\mathcal{S}}_{\alpha}\cap W_{\alpha}\right\rvert+\left\lvert U_{\alpha}\setminus(U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha})\right\rvert+\left\lvert V_{\alpha}\setminus(U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha})\right\rvert\leq\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert
.

Finally, each type 2 edge in
p
can only increase the quantity
\left\lvert{\mathcal{S}}_{\gamma}\cap W_{\gamma}\right\rvert+\left\lvert U_{\gamma}\setminus(U_{\gamma}\cap V_{\gamma})\right\rvert+\left\lvert V_{\gamma}\setminus(U_{\gamma}\cap V_{\gamma})\right\rvert

by at most 2. Therefore, we have the desired inequality
\#_{1}(p)\leq\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert+2\#_{2}(p)
.

Corollary 4.40
.

\#_{2}(p)\geq\frac{\left\lvert p\right\rvert}{3}-\frac{\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert}{3}
.

Proof.

Plug in
\left\lvert p\right\rvert=\#_{1}(p)+\#_{2}(p)
and rearrange.

Finally, we can bound the accumulation on each non-spider by a term which only depends on the parameters of the spider
\alpha
.

Lemma 4.41
.

There are absolute constants
C_{1},C_{2}
so that for all leaves
\gamma
of
W(\alpha)
,

\left\lvert v_{\gamma}\right\rvert\leq(C_{1}\cdot\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert\cdot\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)^{C_{2}\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert}.

Proof.

To bound
\left\lvert v_{\gamma}\right\rvert
we will sum the contributions of all paths
p=(\beta_{0}=\alpha,\dots,\beta_{r}=\gamma)
in
W(\alpha)

starting from
\alpha
and ending at
\gamma
. This path contributes a product of coefficients
c_{\beta}
towards
v_{\gamma}
.

Remark 4.42
.

Here it is important that type 2 edges have stronger bounds on their coefficients
\left\lvert c_{\beta}\right\rvert\leq C\cdot(\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)^{O(1)}/n\ll 1
.

Before we proceed with the proof we establish some convenient notation and recall some facts.
For consecutive shapes
\beta_{i-1},\beta_{i}
(i.e.,
\beta_{i}
is a child of
\beta_{i-1}
),
we denote by
c_{\beta_{i}}
the coefficient from
Corollary

4.34
applied on
\beta_{i-1}
.
By
Proposition

4.38
, the in-degree of
W(\alpha)
can be bounded as
B_{1}~\cdot~(\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)^{B_{2}}
for some constants
B_{1},B_{2}
. Thus,
the number of paths of length
r
ending at
\gamma
is at most
(B_{1}\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)^{B_{2}r}
. Using
Corollary

4.34
, set
B_{1},B_{2}
large enough so that
c_{\beta_{i}}
is at most
B_{1}\cdot(\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)^{B_{2}}
for a type
1
edge (resp.
B_{1}\cdot(\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)^{B_{2}}/n
for a type
2
edge).

\displaystyle\left\lvert v_{\gamma}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p=(\beta_{0}=\alpha,\dots,\beta_{r}=\gamma)\\
\textup{path from $\alpha$ to $\gamma$ in }W(\alpha)\end{subarray}}\prod_{i=1}^{r}\left\lvert c_{\beta_{i}}\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p=(\beta_{0}=\alpha,\dots,\beta_{r}=\gamma)\\
\textup{path from $\alpha$ to $\gamma$ in }W(\alpha)\end{subarray}}\left(B_{1}\cdot(\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)^{B_{2}}\right)^{\#_{1}(p)}\left(B_{1}\cdot(\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)^{B_{2}}/n\right)^{\#_{2}(p)}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p=(\beta_{0}=\alpha,\dots,\beta_{r}=\gamma)\\
\textup{path from $\alpha$ to $\gamma$ in }W(\alpha)\end{subarray}}\left(B_{1}\cdot(\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)^{B_{2}}\right)^{\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert+2\#_{2}(p)}\left(B_{1}\cdot(\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)^{B_{2}}/n\right)^{\#_{2}(p)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{r=0}^{\infty}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}p=(\beta_{0}=\alpha,\dots,\beta_{r}=\gamma)\\
\textup{path from $\alpha$ to $\gamma$ in }W(\alpha)\end{subarray}}\left(B_{1}\cdot(\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)^{B_{2}}\right)^{\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert}\left(B_{1}^{\prime}\cdot(\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)^{B_{2}^{\prime}}/n\right)^{\#_{2}(p)}

for some constants
B_{1}^{\prime},B_{2}^{\prime}

where the first inequality followed by
Corollary

4.34
and the second inequality followed by
Proposition

4.39
.
We split the above sum into two sums,
r\leq 3|E(\alpha)|
and
r>3|E(\alpha)|
. For
r\leq 3\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert
, upper bounding the
\#_{2}(p)
term by 1 and upper bounding
the number of paths by
(B_{1}\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)^{B_{2}r}
gives a
bound of
(B_{1}^{\prime\prime}\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)^{B_{2}^{\prime\prime}\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert}
for some constants
B_{1}^{\prime\prime},B_{2}^{\prime\prime}
.
For larger
r
, we lower bound

\#_{2}(p)\geq r/9=\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert/3
using
Corollary

4.40
. Applying the same bound on the number of paths,
the total contribution of the terms corresponding to larger
r
is bounded by
1 using the power of
n
in the denominator (assuming
\delta,\tau
are
small enough).

We define the result of all this spider killing to be a new matrix
{\mathcal{M}}^{+}
.

Definition 4.43
.

Define the matrix
{\mathcal{M}}^{+}
as the result of killing all the spiders,

{\mathcal{M}}^{+}:={\mathcal{M}}-\displaystyle\sum_{\text{spiders }\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}\left(M_{\alpha}-\sum_{\text{leaves }\gamma\text{ of }W(\alpha)}v_{\gamma}M_{\gamma}\right)

4
Finishing the proof

The final step of the proof is to argue that, after the spider killing
process is completed, the newly created non-spider terms in
{\mathcal{M}}^{+}
also
have small norm. Towards this, we would like to prove a statement similar
to
Corollary

4.11
. In that proof, we used special
structural properties of the non-spiders in
{\mathcal{L}}
to
prove that non-spiders in the pseudocalibration were negligible.
But now, the non-spiders in
{\mathcal{M}}^{+}
need not have the properties of

{\mathcal{L}}
– for instance, there could be circle vertices of degree
2
or
isolated vertices. To handle the potentially larger norms, we will use
that the coefficients of these new non-spider terms
\beta
come with the
coefficients
\lambda_{\alpha}
of the spider terms
\alpha
in whose web
they lie. Since
\alpha
has more vertices/edges than
\beta
, the power of
\frac{1}{n}
in
\lambda_{\alpha}
is larger than the “expected pseudocalibration” coefficient of
\eta^{\left\lvert U_{\beta}\right\rvert+\left\lvert V_{\beta}\right\rvert}\cdot\frac{1}{n^{\left\lvert E(\beta)\right\rvert/2}}
.
We prove that these extra factors of
\frac{1}{n}
are enough to overpower isolated
vertices or a smaller vertex separator using a careful
charging argument.

Lemma 4.44
.

If
\beta
is a nontrivial non-spider and
\beta\in W(\alpha)
for some spider
\alpha\in{\mathcal{L}}
, then

\eta^{\left\lvert U_{\alpha}\right\rvert+\left\lvert V_{\alpha}\right\rvert}\cdot\frac{1}{n^{\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert/2}}\cdot n^{\frac{w(V(\beta))-w(S_{\min})+w(W_{iso})}{2}}\leq\eta^{\left\lvert U_{\beta}\right\rvert+\left\lvert V_{\beta}\right\rvert}\cdot\frac{1}{n^{\Omega(\varepsilon\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)}}

where
S_{min}
and
W_{iso}
are the minimum vertex separator of
\beta
and the set of isolated vertices of
V(\beta)\setminus(U_{\beta}\cup V_{\beta})
respectively.

Proof.

We start by giving the idea of the proof. Suppose we try to use the same
distribution scheme as in the proof of
Lemma

4.7
. It doesn’t work
for two reasons. Firstly, the circle vertices in
\beta
still have even
degree, which follows from
Remark

4.18
, but now, they could have
degrees
0
or
2
. For the previous distribution scheme to go through, we
needed them to have degree at least
4
which gave the necessary edge decay
to handle the norm bounds. Secondly, the square vertices can now have degree

0
hence getting no decay from the edges.

The first issue is relatively easy to handle. Since
\beta
was obtained by
collapsing
\alpha
, the circle vertices of degrees
0
or
2
in
\beta

must have had degree at least
4
in
\alpha
to begin with. Hence, we can
fix a particular sequence of collapses from
\alpha
to
\beta
and then
assume for the sake of analysis that the removed edges are still present. In
this case, the same charging argument as in
Lemma

4.7
would go
through. This is made formal by looking at the sequence of improper
collapses of this chain of collapses.

To handle the second issue, let’s analyze more carefully how degree
0

square vertices appear. Fix a sequence of collapses from
\alpha
to
\beta

and consider a specific step where
\gamma
collapsed to
\gamma^{\prime}
and a
square vertex of degree
0
was formed. Let the two square vertices that
collapsed in
\gamma
be
\scalebox{0.85}{\footnotesize\hbox to9.68pt{\vbox to9.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.83905pt\lower-4.83905pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.42 6.42 L -6.42 6.42 L -6.42 -6.42 L 6.42 -6.42 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.7803pt}{-2.64435pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}},\scalebox{0.85}{\footnotesize\hbox to11.49pt{\vbox to11.49pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.7467pt\lower-5.7467pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 7.67 7.67 L -7.67 7.67 L -7.67 -7.67 L 7.67 -7.67 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.28262pt}{-1.86658pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}
and let the square vertex
of degree
0
that formed in
\gamma^{\prime}
be

k

. In light of

Remark

4.18
, since

k

has degree
0
, it must not be in

(U_{\gamma^{\prime}}\cup V_{\gamma^{\prime}})\setminus(U_{\gamma^{\prime}}\cap V_{\gamma^{\prime}})
and
hence,
U_{\gamma^{\prime}}(\scalebox{0.85}{\footnotesize\hbox to12.15pt{\vbox to12.15pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.07294pt\lower-6.07294pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.13 8.13 L -8.13 8.13 L -8.13 -8.13 L 8.13 -8.13 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.6528pt}{-2.77777pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})=V_{\gamma^{\prime}}(\scalebox{0.85}{\footnotesize\hbox to12.15pt{\vbox to12.15pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.07294pt\lower-6.07294pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.6528pt}{-2.77777pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})=0
or

.

When such a collapse happens, observe that
|U_{\gamma}|+|V_{\gamma}|\geq|U_{\gamma^{\prime}}|+|V_{\gamma^{\prime}}|+2
. This is precisely where the decay from
our normalization factor
\eta=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}
kicks in. This
inequality means that an extra decay factor of
\eta^{2}=\frac{1}{n}
is
available to us when we compare to the ”expected pseudocalibration”
coefficient of
\beta
. We will use this factor to charge the new square
vertex of degree
0
.

We now make these ideas formal.

Let
Q=U_{\beta}\cap V_{\beta},P=(U_{\beta}\cup V_{\beta})\setminus Q
and
let
P^{\prime}
be the set of degree
1
square vertices in
\beta
that are not in

S_{min}
. Let
s_{0}
be the number of degree
0
square vertices in

V(\beta)\setminus Q
. All the square vertices outside
P^{\prime}\cup Q\cup S_{min}
have degree at least
2
, let there be
s_{\geq 2}
of them.

Because of parity constraints,
Remark

4.18
, and because there are no
circle vertices in
U_{\beta}\cup V_{\beta}
, all circle vertices have even
degree in
\beta
. Let
c_{0}
be the number of degree
0
circle vertices in

\beta
. Let
c_{2},c_{\geq 4}
be the number of degree
2
circle vertices and
the number of circle vertices of degree at least
4
in
V(\beta)\setminus S_{min}
respectively. Then, we have

n^{\frac{w(V(\beta))-w(S_{\min})+w(W_{iso})}{2}}\leq n^{\frac{|P^{\prime}|+s_{\geq 2}+(1.5-\varepsilon)(c_{2}+c_{\geq 4})}{2}}\cdot n^{s_{0}+(1.5-\varepsilon)c_{0}}

Using
\eta=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}
, it suffices to show

\displaystyle\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert+

\displaystyle(|U_{\alpha}|+|V_{\alpha}|-|U_{\beta}|-|V_{\beta}|)

\displaystyle\geq|P^{\prime}|+s_{\geq 2}+(1.5-\varepsilon)(c_{2}+c_{\geq 4})+2s_{0}+2(1.5-\varepsilon)c_{0}+\Omega(\varepsilon\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)

There can be many ways to collapse
\alpha
to
\beta
, fix any one. We first use a charging argument for the degree
0
square vertices.

Lemma 4.45
.

|U_{\alpha}|+|V_{\alpha}|-|U_{\beta}|-|V_{\beta}|\geq 2s_{0}

Proof.

In the collapse process, in each step, a vertex
\scalebox{0.85}{\footnotesize\hbox to9.68pt{\vbox to9.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.83905pt\lower-4.83905pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.42 6.42 L -6.42 6.42 L -6.42 -6.42 L 6.42 -6.42 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.7803pt}{-2.64435pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\in U_{\gamma}\setminus(U_{\gamma}\cap V_{\gamma})
or
\scalebox{0.85}{\footnotesize\hbox to9.68pt{\vbox to9.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.83905pt\lower-4.83905pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.42 6.42 L -6.42 6.42 L -6.42 -6.42 L 6.42 -6.42 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.7803pt}{-2.64435pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\in V_{\gamma}\setminus(U_{\gamma}\cap V_{\gamma})
of degree
1
in an intermediate shape
\gamma
collapses with another square vertex

k

. We have that
|U_{\gamma}|+|V_{\gamma}|
decreases precisely when

i

collapses with
\scalebox{0.85}{\footnotesize\hbox to12.15pt{\vbox to12.15pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.07294pt\lower-6.07294pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.13 8.13 L -8.13 8.13 L -8.13 -8.13 L 8.13 -8.13 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.6528pt}{-2.77777pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\in U_{\gamma}
(resp.
\scalebox{0.85}{\footnotesize\hbox to12.15pt{\vbox to12.15pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.07294pt\lower-6.07294pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.13 8.13 L -8.13 8.13 L -8.13 -8.13 L 8.13 -8.13 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.6528pt}{-2.77777pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}\in V_{\gamma}
). In either case, the quantity decreases by exactly
2
which we allocate to this new merged vertex. Each degree
0
square vertex in
V(\beta)\setminus Q
must have arisen from a collapse, and hence must have had at least an additive quantity of
2
allocated to it. This proves that
|U_{\alpha}|+|V_{\alpha}|-|U_{\beta}|-|V_{\beta}|\geq 2s_{0}
.

We will now prove a structural lemma.

Lemma 4.46
.

Any vertex

u

that has degree at least
2
in
V(\beta)\setminus S_{min}
is adjacent to at most
1
vertex of
P^{\prime}
.

Proof.

Observe that

u

cannot be adjacent to
3
vertices in
P^{\prime}
because otherwise, at least
2
of them would be in
U_{\beta}\setminus Q
or in
V_{\beta}\setminus Q
which means
\beta
would be a spider which is a contradiction. If

u

is adjacent to
2
vertices in
P^{\prime}
, then one of them is in
U_{\beta}\setminus Q
and the other is in
V_{\beta}\setminus Q
respectively. Since both of these vertices are not in
S_{min}
, it follows that

u

is in
S_{min}
since there is no path from
U_{\beta}
to
V_{\beta}
that doesn’t pass through
S_{min}
. This is a contradiction. Therefore,

u

is adjacent to at most
1
vertex in
P^{\prime}
.

This lemma immediately implies
|P^{\prime}|\leq c_{2}+c_{\geq 4}
.

To account for edges of
\alpha
that are not in
\beta
, we let

\widetilde{\beta}
be the result of improperly collapsing
\alpha
to

\beta
; note that
\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert=\left\lvert E(\widetilde{\beta})\right\rvert
.
We call the edges that disappeared when properly collapsing “phantom” edges.
Let
\deg_{\widetilde{\beta}}(\scalebox{0.85}{\footnotesize\hbox to9.68pt{\vbox to9.68pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 4.83905pt\lower-4.83905pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{}{}{}{{}}{}{{}{}}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{{}{}}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 6.42 6.42 L -6.42 6.42 L -6.42 -6.42 L 6.42 -6.42 Z}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-2.7803pt}{-2.64435pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})
(resp.
\deg_{\widetilde{\beta}}(\scalebox{0.85}{\footnotesize\hbox to12.78pt{\vbox to12.78pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.38823pt\lower-6.38823pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.56 0 C 8.56 4.73 4.73 8.56 0 8.56 C -4.73 8.56 -8.56 4.73 -8.56 0 C -8.56 -4.73 -4.73 -8.56 0 -8.56 C 4.73 -8.56 8.56 -4.73 8.56 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.794pt}{-1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})
) denote the degree of vertex

i

(resp.

u

) in
\widetilde{\beta}
. Observe that any circle vertex

u

in
V(\beta)
has
deg_{\widetilde{\beta}}(\scalebox{0.85}{\footnotesize\hbox to12.78pt{\vbox to12.78pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.38823pt\lower-6.38823pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.56 0 C 8.56 4.73 4.73 8.56 0 8.56 C -4.73 8.56 -8.56 4.73 -8.56 0 C -8.56 -4.73 -4.73 -8.56 0 -8.56 C 4.73 -8.56 8.56 -4.73 8.56 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.794pt}{-1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})\geq 4
.

Lemma 4.47
.

\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert\geq|P^{\prime}|+s_{\geq 2}+(1.5-\varepsilon)(c_{2}+c_{\geq 4})+2(1.5-\varepsilon)c_{0}+\Omega(\varepsilon\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)

Proof.

We will use the following charging scheme. Each edge of
\beta
incident on
P^{\prime}
allocates
1
to the incident square vertex, which is in
P^{\prime}
. Every other edge of
\beta
allocates
\frac{1}{2}
to the incident square vertex and
\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\varepsilon}{10}
to the incident circle vertex. Each phantom edge allocates
1-\frac{\varepsilon}{10}
to the incident circle vertex

u

. So, a total of
\frac{\varepsilon}{10}(\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert-|P^{\prime}|)
has not been allocated.

All square vertices in
P^{\prime}
have been allocated a value of
1
. And observe that all square vertices of degree at least
2
in
\beta
have been allocated at least
1
from the incident edges of
\beta
, for a total value of
s_{\geq 2}
. So, the square vertices get a total allocation of at least
|P^{\prime}|+s_{\geq 2}
.

Consider any degree-
0
circle vertex

u

in
V(\beta)
. It must be incident to at least
4
phantom edges and hence, must be allocated at least a value of
4(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{10})>2(1.5-\varepsilon)
. Hence, the degree-
0
circle vertices in
V(\beta
) have a total allocation of at least
2(1.5-\varepsilon)c_{0}
.

Suppose the degree of

u

in
V(\beta)
is
2
. Then, it is incident on at least
2
phantom edges.
By
Lemma

4.46
, it is also adjacent to at most one vertex of
P^{\prime}
and so, must have been allocated a value of at least
2(1-\frac{\varepsilon}{10})+(deg_{\widetilde{\beta}}(\scalebox{0.85}{\footnotesize\hbox to12.78pt{\vbox to12.78pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.38823pt\lower-6.38823pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.56 0 C 8.56 4.73 4.73 8.56 0 8.56 C -4.73 8.56 -8.56 4.73 -8.56 0 C -8.56 -4.73 -4.73 -8.56 0 -8.56 C 4.73 -8.56 8.56 -4.73 8.56 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.794pt}{-1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})-3)(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\varepsilon}{10})
. This is at least
1.5-\varepsilon+\frac{\varepsilon}{10}
.

Suppose the degree of

u

in
V(\beta)
is at least
4
. By
Lemma

4.46
, it is adjacent to at most one vertex of
P^{\prime}
. Then it must have been allocated a value of at least
(deg_{\widetilde{\beta}}(\scalebox{0.85}{\footnotesize\hbox to12.78pt{\vbox to12.78pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.38823pt\lower-6.38823pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.56 0 C 8.56 4.73 4.73 8.56 0 8.56 C -4.73 8.56 -8.56 4.73 -8.56 0 C -8.56 -4.73 -4.73 -8.56 0 -8.56 C 4.73 -8.56 8.56 -4.73 8.56 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.794pt}{-1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})-1)(\frac{1}{2}-\frac{\varepsilon}{10})
. Using
deg_{\widetilde{\beta}}(\scalebox{0.85}{\footnotesize\hbox to12.78pt{\vbox to12.78pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 6.38823pt\lower-6.38823pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{{{}}}{{}}{}{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}{}{}{}{}{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 8.56 0 C 8.56 4.73 4.73 8.56 0 8.56 C -4.73 8.56 -8.56 4.73 -8.56 0 C -8.56 -4.73 -4.73 -8.56 0 -8.56 C 4.73 -8.56 8.56 -4.73 8.56 0 Z M 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-3.794pt}{-1.72221pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
}
\lxSVG@closescope {{{}}}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}})\geq 4
, this is at least
1.5-\varepsilon+\frac{\varepsilon}{10}
.

This implies

\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert\geq|P^{\prime}|+s_{\geq 2}+2(1.5-\varepsilon)c_{0}+(1.5-\varepsilon+\frac{\varepsilon}{10})(c_{2}+c_{\geq 4})+\frac{\varepsilon}{10}(\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert-|P^{\prime}|)

Using
|P^{\prime}|\leq c_{2}+c_{\geq 4}
completes the proof.

Adding
Lemma

4.45
and
Lemma

4.47
, we get the result.

Corollary 4.48
.

If
\beta
is a nontrivial non-spider and
\beta\in W(\alpha)
for some spider
\alpha\in{\mathcal{L}}
, then

\eta^{\left\lvert U_{\alpha}\right\rvert+\left\lvert V_{\alpha}\right\rvert}\cdot\frac{1}{n^{\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert/2}}\left\lVert M_{\beta}\right\rVert\leq\eta^{\left\lvert U_{\beta}\right\rvert+\left\lvert V_{\beta}\right\rvert}\cdot\frac{1}{n^{\Omega(\varepsilon\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)}}

Proof.

From
Lemma

6.3
, we have

\left\lVert M_{\beta}\right\rVert\leq 2\cdot\left(\left\lvert V(\beta)\right\rvert\cdot(1+\left\lvert E(\beta)\right\rvert)\cdot\log(n)\right)^{C\cdot(\left\lvert V_{rel}(\beta)\right\rvert+\left\lvert E(\beta)\right\rvert)}\cdot n^{\frac{w(V(\beta))-w(S_{\min})+w(W_{iso})}{2}}

We have
\left\lvert V(\beta)\right\rvert\cdot(1+\left\lvert E(\beta)\right\rvert)\cdot\log(n)\leq n^{O(\tau)}
. Also,
|V_{rel}(\beta)|\leq 2(|E(\alpha)|+|E(\beta)|)
since all the degree
0
vertices in
V_{rel}(\beta)
would have had vertices of
V_{rel}(\alpha)
collapse into it in the chain of collapses and there are no degree
0
vertices in
V_{rel}(\alpha)
. Finally, since
|E(\alpha)|\geq|E(\beta)|
, the factor

2\cdot(\left\lvert V(\beta)\right\rvert\cdot(1+\left\lvert E(\beta)\right\rvert)\cdot\log(n))^{C\cdot(\left\lvert V_{rel}(\beta)\right\rvert+\left\lvert E(\beta)\right\rvert)}
can be absorbed into
\frac{1}{n^{\Omega(\varepsilon\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)}}
. The result follows from
Lemma

4.44
.

Proposition 4.49
.

If
\beta
is a trivial shape,
\lambda_{\beta}^{+}=\lambda_{\beta}
.

Proof.

A trivial shape cannot appear in
W(\alpha)
for any
\alpha
, since every collapse of a spider always keeps its circle vertices around.

Lemma 4.50
.

For
k,l\in\{0,1,\dots,D/2\}
, let
{\mathcal{B}}_{k,l}
denote the set of nontrivial non-spiders on block
(k,l)
. Then

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k,l}}\left\lvert\lambda_{\beta}^{+}\right\rvert\left\lVert M_{\beta}\right\rVert\leq\eta^{k+l}\cdot\frac{1}{n^{\Omega(\varepsilon)}}

Proof.

\displaystyle\displaystyle\sum_{\beta\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k,l}}\left\lVert\lambda_{\beta}^{+}M_{\beta}\right\rVert\leq

\displaystyle\sum_{\beta\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k,l}}\left\lvert\lambda_{\beta}\right\rvert\left\lVert M_{\beta}\right\rVert+\sum_{\beta\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k,l}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{spiders }\alpha:\\
\beta\in W(\alpha)\end{subarray}}\left\lvert v_{\beta}\right\rvert\left\lvert\lambda_{\alpha}\right\rvert\left\lVert M_{\beta}\right\rVert

To bound the first term, we checked previously in
Corollary

4.14
that the total norm of nontrivial non-spiders appearing in the pseudocalibration (i.e. this term) is
\eta^{k+l}o_{n}(1)
. For the second term, via
Lemma

4.41
we have a bound on the accumulations
v_{\gamma}
of one spider on one non-spider, so it is at most

\leq\sum_{\beta\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k,l}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{spiders }\alpha:\\
\beta\in W(\alpha)\end{subarray}}(C_{1}\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert\cdot\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)^{C_{2}\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert}\cdot\left\lvert\lambda_{\alpha}\right\rvert\left\lVert M_{\beta}\right\rVert.

Use the bound on the coefficients
\left\lvert\lambda_{\alpha}\right\rvert
,
Proposition

4.13
,

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{\beta\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k,l}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{spiders }\alpha:\\
\beta\in W(\alpha)\end{subarray}}(C_{1}\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert\cdot\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)^{C_{2}\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert}\cdot\eta^{\left\lvert U_{\alpha}\right\rvert+\left\lvert V_{\alpha}\right\rvert}\cdot\frac{\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert^{3\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert}}{n^{\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert/2}}\cdot\left\lVert M_{\beta}\right\rVert

Invoking the norm bound for non-spiders which are collapses,
Corollary

4.48
,

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{k+l}\cdot\sum_{\beta\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k,l}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{spiders }\alpha:\\
\beta\in W(\alpha)\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{C_{1}\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert\cdot\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert}{n^{\Omega(\varepsilon)}}\right)^{C_{2}^{\prime}\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert}

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{k+l}\cdot\sum_{\beta\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k,l}}\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\text{spiders }\alpha:\\
\beta\in W(\alpha)\end{subarray}}\left(\frac{C_{1}n^{\tau}\cdot n^{\tau}}{n^{\Omega(\varepsilon)}}\right)^{C_{2}^{\prime}\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert}.

Bound the sum over all spiders by the sum over all shapes. By
Proposition

4.12
, the number of shapes with
i
edges is
n^{O(\tau(i+1))}
. Summing by the number of edges, observe that
\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert\geq\max(\left\lvert E(\beta)\right\rvert,2)
since spiders always have at least
2
edges.

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{k+l}\sum_{\beta\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k,l}}\displaystyle\sum_{i=\max(\left\lvert E(\beta)\right\rvert,2)}^{\infty}n^{O(\tau(i+1))}\cdot\left(\frac{C_{1}n^{\tau}\cdot n^{\tau}}{n^{\Omega(\varepsilon)}}\right)^{C_{2}^{\prime}i}

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{k+l}\sum_{\beta\in{\mathcal{B}}_{k,l}}\frac{1}{n^{\Omega(\varepsilon\max(\left\lvert E(\beta)\right\rvert,2))}}

\displaystyle\leq\eta^{k+l}\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\frac{n^{O(\delta(i+1))}}{n^{\Omega(\varepsilon\max(i,2))}}

\displaystyle=\eta^{k+l}\cdot\frac{1}{n^{\Omega(\varepsilon)}}\qquad\hbox{\vrule width=6.0pt,height=6.0pt,depth=0.0pt}

Corollary 4.51
.

For
k\in\{0,\dots,D/2\}
, the
(k,k)
block of
{\mathcal{M}}^{+}
has minimum singular value at least
\eta^{2k}(1-\frac{1}{n^{\Omega(\varepsilon)}})
, and for
k,l\in\{0,\dots,D/2\},l\neq k
, the
(k,l)
off-diagonal block has norm at most
\eta^{k+l}\cdot\frac{1}{n^{\Omega(\varepsilon)}}
.

Proof.

By
Proposition

4.49
the identity matrix appears on the
(k,k)
blocks with coefficient
\eta^{2k}
. By construction,
{\mathcal{M}}^{+}
has no spider shapes. By
Lemma

4.50
, the total norm of the non-spider shapes on the
(k,l)
block is at most
\eta^{k+l}\cdot\frac{1}{n^{\Omega(\varepsilon)}}
.

Theorem 4.52
.

W.h.p.
{\mathcal{M}}_{fix}\succeq 0
.

Proof.

For any
x\in\nullspace({\mathcal{M}}_{fix})
, we of course have
x^{\intercal}{\mathcal{M}}_{fix}x=0
.
For any
x\perp\nullspace({\mathcal{M}}_{fix})
with
\left\lVert x\right\rVert_{2}=1
,

\displaystyle x^{\intercal}{\mathcal{M}}_{fix}x

\displaystyle=x^{\intercal}({\mathcal{M}}+{\mathcal{E}})x

\displaystyle=x^{\intercal}{\mathcal{M}}^{+}x+x^{\intercal}\left(\displaystyle\sum_{\text{spiders }\alpha}\lambda_{\alpha}\left({\mathcal{M}}_{\alpha}-\sum_{\text{leaves }\gamma\text{ of }W(\alpha)}v_{\gamma}M_{\gamma}\right)\right)x+x^{\intercal}{\mathcal{E}}x

\displaystyle=x^{\intercal}({\mathcal{M}}^{+}+{\mathcal{E}})x

where the last equality follows from
Proposition

4.37
.
Because the norm bound on
{\mathcal{E}}
is significantly less than
\eta^{D}=n^{-n^{\delta}}
(see
[
sklowerbounds
]
), the bound on the norm of each block of
{\mathcal{M}}^{+}

in
Corollary

4.51
also applies to the blocks of
{\mathcal{M}}^{+}+{\mathcal{E}}
. Therefore,
we use
Lemma

4.2
to conclude
{\mathcal{M}}^{+}+{\mathcal{E}}\succeq 0
and the above expression is nonnegative.

5
Sherrington-Kirkpatrick Lower Bounds

Here, we prove
Theorem

1.5
and
Theorem

1.2
.

Recall that in the Planted Boolean Vector problem, we wish to optimize

\OPT(V):=\frac{1}{n}\max_{b\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}}b^{\intercal}\Pi_{V}b,

where
V
is a uniformly random
p
-dimensional subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

See
1.5

Proof.

We wish to produce an SoS solution
\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}
on boolean variables
b_{1},\ldots,b_{n}
such that
\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}[b^{\intercal}\Pi_{V}b]=n
.
Instead of sampling a uniformly random
p
-dimensional subspace
V
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we first sample
d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}
i.i.d.
p
-dimensional
Gaussian vectors from
\mathcal{N}(0,I)
, then form an
n
-by-
p
matrix
A
with rows
d_{1},\dots,d_{n}
, and finally take

V
to be the span of the columns of
A
. Since the columns of
A
are isotropic i.i.d. random Gaussian vectors, we have
that
V
is a uniform
p
-dimensional subspace
5
5

5

Except for a zero measure event.
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

We will consider
V
as the input for the Planted Boolean Vector problem
while the vectors
d_{1},\dots,d_{n}
will be used to construct a pseudoexpectation operator for the Planted Affine Planes
problem
6
6

6

Note that the vectors
d_{u}
are not “given” in the Planted Boolean Vector problem, though the construction of
\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}
is not required to be algorithmic in any sense anyway.
.
Since
n\leq p^{3/2-\Omega(\varepsilon)}
, by
Theorem

1.4
, for all
\delta\leq c\varepsilon
for a constant
c>0
,
w.h.p.
, there exists a degree-
n^{\delta}
pseudoexpectation operator
\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}^{\prime}
on formal variables
v=(v_{1},\dots,v_{p})
such that
\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}^{\prime}[\langle v,d_{u}\rangle^{2}]=1

for every
u\in[n]
.

Define
\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}
by
\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}[b_{u}]:=\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}^{\prime}[\langle v,d_{u}\rangle]
for all
u\in[n]
and extending it to all polynomials on
\{b_{u}\}
by
multilinearity. This is well defined because
\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}^{\prime}[\langle v,d_{u}\rangle^{2}]=1
. Note that
\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}
is a valid pseudoexpectation operator
of the same degree as
\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}^{\prime}
. Finally, observe that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n}\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}[b^{\intercal}\Pi_{V}b]=\frac{1}{n}\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}^{\prime}[v^{\intercal}A^{\intercal}\Pi_{V}Av]=\frac{1}{n}\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}^{\prime}[v^{\intercal}A^{\intercal}Av]=1.

Now we prove lower bounds for the Sherrington-Kirkpatrick problem,
using a reduction and proof due to
[
mohanty2020lifting
]
. We include it here
for completeness. Recall that the SK problem is to
compute

\OPT(W):=\max_{x\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}}x^{\intercal}Wx,

where
W
is sampled from
\GOE(n)
.

See
1.2

We will use the following standard results from random matrix theory of
\GOE(n)
.

Fact 5.1
.

Let
\lambda_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{n}
be the eigenvalues of
W\sim\GOE(n)
with corresponding normalized eigenvectors
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
.
Then,

1.

For every
p\in[n]
, the span of
w_{1},\ldots,w_{p}
is a uniformly random
p
-dimensional subspace of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
(see e.g.
[
OVW16
, Section 2]
).

2.

W.h.p.,
\lambda_{n^{0.67}}\geq(2-\operatorname{o}(1))\sqrt{n}
(Corollary of Wigner’s semicircle law
[
Wig93
]
)

Proof of

Theorem

1.2
:

Let
p=n^{0.67}
and
W\sim\GOE(n)
. Let
\lambda_{1}\geq\ldots\geq\lambda_{n}
be the eigenvalues of
W
with corresponding orthonormal set of
eigenvectors
w_{1},\ldots,w_{n}
. By
5.1
, we have that
\lambda_{p}\geq(2-\operatorname{o}(1))\sqrt{n}
and that
w_{1},\ldots,w_{p}
span a
uniformly random
p
-dimensional subspace
V
of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
.

We consider
V
as the input of the Boolean Planted Vector problem and by
Theorem

1.5
, for some constant
\delta>0
,

w.h.p.
there exists a degree-
n^{\delta}
pseudoexpectation operator
\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}
such that
\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}[x_{i}^{2}]=1
and

\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}[\sum_{i=1}^{p}\langle x,w_{i}\rangle^{2}]=\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}[x^{\intercal}\Pi_{V}x]=n
. Now,

\displaystyle\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}[x^{\intercal}Wx]

\displaystyle=\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\lambda_{i}\langle x,w_{i}\rangle^{2}]

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{p}\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}[x^{\intercal}\Pi_{V}x]-\left\lvert\lambda_{n}\right\rvert\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}[\sum_{i=p+1}^{n}\langle x,w_{i}\rangle^{2}]

\displaystyle\geq(2-\operatorname{o}(1))n^{3/2}-\left\lvert\lambda_{n}\right\rvert\widetilde{{\mathbb{E}}}[\langle x,x\rangle-\sum_{i=1}^{p}\langle x,w_{i}\rangle^{2}]

\displaystyle=(2-\operatorname{o}(1))n^{3/2}.

Remark 5.2
.

Using the same proof as above, we can obtain
Theorem

1.2
even if we were only able to prove SoS lower
bounds for Planted Affine Planes for some
m=\omega(n)
. So, pushing the value of
m
up to
n^{3/2-\varepsilon}
, which
is
Theorem

1.4
, offers only a modest improvement.

6
Omitted technical details

1
Norm Bounds

The precise norm bounds we use come from applying the trace power method
in
[
ahn2016graph
]
, but qualitatively, the bounds from
Chapter

1
also work. The paper
[
ahn2016graph
]
uses a slightly different
definition of matrix index. They define a
matrix index piece

as a tuple of distinct elements from either
{\mathcal{C}}_{m}
or
{\mathcal{S}}_{n}

along with a fixed integer denoting multiplicity. A matrix index is
then a set of matrix index pieces. Our graph matrix
M_{\alpha}
appears
as a submatrix of those matrices: for a given set of square vertices,
order the squares in increasing order in a tuple, and assign it
multiplicity 1. Hence the same norm bounds apply.

Boolean norm bounds:

Lemma 6.1
.

Let
V_{rel}(\alpha):=V(\alpha)\setminus(U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha})
. There is a universal constant
C
such that the following norm bound holds for all proper shapes
\alpha
w.h.p.:

\left\lVert M_{\alpha}\right\rVert\leq 2\cdot\left(\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert\cdot\log(n)\right)^{C\cdot\left\lvert V_{rel}(\alpha)\right\rvert}\cdot n^{\frac{w(V(\alpha))-w(S_{\min})+w(W_{iso})}{2}}

Proof.

From Corollary 8.13 of
[
ahn2016graph
]
, with probability at least
1-\varepsilon
for a fixed shape
\alpha
,

\left\lVert M_{\alpha}\right\rVert\leq 2\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert^{\left\lvert V_{rel}(\alpha)\right\rvert}\cdot\left(6e\left\lceil\frac{\log\left(\frac{n^{w(S_{\min})}}{\varepsilon}\right)}{6\left\lvert V_{rel}(\alpha)\right\rvert}\right\rceil\right)^{\left\lvert V_{rel}(\alpha)\right\rvert}\cdot n^{\frac{w(V(\alpha))-w(S_{\min})+w(W_{iso})}{2}}

Letting
N_{k}
be the number of distinct shapes on
k
vertices (either
circles or squares), we apply the corollary with
\varepsilon=1/(mnN_{\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert})
. Union bounding, the failure probability across
all shapes of size
k
is at most
1/mn
, and since the number of
vertices in a shape is at most
m+n\leq 2m
, we have a bound that
holds with high probability for all shapes. It remains to simplify the
exact bound.

Proposition 6.2
.

N_{k}\leq 8^{k}2^{k^{2}}

Proof.

The following process forms all shapes on
k
vertices: starting from
k
formal variables, assign each variable to be either a circle or a square, decide whether each variable is in
U_{\alpha}
and/or
V_{\alpha}
, then among the
k^{2}
variable pairs put any number of edges.

We also bound
n^{w(S_{\min})}\leq(mn)^{\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert}
.

\displaystyle\left\lVert M_{\alpha}\right\rVert

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert^{\left\lvert V_{rel}(\alpha)\right\rvert}\cdot\left(6e\left\lceil\frac{\log\left(n^{w(S_{\min})}\cdot mnN_{\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert}\right)}{6\left\lvert V_{rel}(\alpha)\right\rvert}\right\rceil\right)^{\left\lvert V_{rel}(\alpha)\right\rvert}\cdot n^{\frac{w(V(\alpha))-w(S_{\min})+w(W_{iso})}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert^{\left\lvert V_{rel}(\alpha)\right\rvert}\cdot\left(12e\log\left(n^{w(S_{\min})}\cdot mnN_{\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert}\right)\right)^{\left\lvert V_{rel}(\alpha)\right\rvert}\cdot n^{\frac{w(V(\alpha))-w(S_{\min})+w(W_{iso})}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert^{\left\lvert V_{rel}(\alpha)\right\rvert}\cdot\left(12e\log\left((mn)^{\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert}\cdot mn\cdot 8^{\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert}2^{\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert^{2}}\right)\right)^{\left\lvert V_{rel}(\alpha)\right\rvert}\cdot n^{\frac{w(V(\alpha))-w(S_{\min})+w(W_{iso})}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert^{\left\lvert V_{rel}(\alpha)\right\rvert}\cdot\left(100e\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert^{2}\log\left(mn\right)\right)^{\left\lvert V_{rel}(\alpha)\right\rvert}\cdot n^{\frac{w(V(\alpha))-w(S_{\min})+w(W_{iso})}{2}}

\displaystyle\leq 2\cdot\left(\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert\cdot\log(mn)\right)^{3\cdot\left\lvert V_{rel}(\alpha)\right\rvert}\cdot n^{\frac{w(V(\alpha))-w(S_{\min})+w(W_{iso})}{2}}

Note that we now assume
m\leq n^{2}
.

We have the following norm bound for Hermite shapes. For a Hermite shape
\alpha
, define the
total size
to be
\left\lvert U_{\alpha}\right\rvert+\left\lvert V_{\alpha}\right\rvert+\left\lvert W_{\alpha}\right\rvert+\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert
.

Lemma 6.3
.

Let
V_{rel}(\alpha):=V(\alpha)\setminus(U_{\alpha}\cap V_{\alpha})
as sets. There is a universal constant
C
such that the following norm bound holds for all proper shapes
\alpha
with total size at most
n
w.h.p.:

\left\lVert M_{\alpha}\right\rVert\leq 2\cdot\left(\left\lvert V(\alpha)\right\rvert\cdot(1+\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)\cdot\log(n)\right)^{C\cdot(\left\lvert V_{rel}(\alpha)\right\rvert+\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)}\cdot n^{\frac{w(V(\alpha))-w(S_{\min})+w(W_{iso})}{2}}

The proof performs the same calculation starting from
[
ahn2016graph
, Corollary 8.15]
. Note that in our notation,
l(\alpha)=\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert
. There is a further difference which is that
[
ahn2016graph
]
uses normalized Hermite polynomials whereas we use unnormalized Hermite polynomials; this contributes the additional term
\prod_{e\in E(\alpha)}l(e)!\leq(1+\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert)^{\left\lvert E(\alpha)\right\rvert}
. We must replace Proposition
6.2
with the following:

Proposition 6.4
.

The number of Hermite shapes with total size
k
is at most
k2^{k}(k+1)^{2k+k^{2}}
.

Proof.

Such a shape has at most
k
distinct variable vertices. Each of these is either a circle or a square. Each variable can be in
U_{\alpha}
with multiplicity between 0 and (at most)
k
, and also in
V_{\alpha}
with multiplicity between 0 and
k
. The
k^{2}
possible pairs of vertices can have edge multiplicity in
E(\alpha)
between 0 and
k
.

2
Properties of
e(k)

In this section, we establish some properties of the
e(k)
used in the
analysis. Recall that
e(k)={\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}\dots x_{k}\right]
where

\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})\coloneqq\{x\in\{\pm 1\}^{n}\mid\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}=\sqrt{n}\}
.

Claim 6.5
.

e(2)=0
.

Proof.

Fix
y\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})
. Note that
(\sum_{i=1}^{n}y_{i})^{2}=n
implying

\sum_{i<j}y_{i}y_{j}=0
. Using this fact, we get

{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}x_{2}\right]={\mathbb{E}}_{\sigma\in S_{n}}y_{\sigma(1)}y_{\sigma(2)}=0,

concluding the proof.

Definition 6.6
.

We say that a tuple
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k})
of non-negative integers is a partition of
k

provided
\sum_{i=1}^{k}\lambda_{i}=k
and
\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{k}
. We use the notation
\lambda\vdash k

to denote a partition of
k
. We refer to
\lambda_{i}
as a row/part of
\lambda
.

In the following, we will dealing with polynomials that can be indexed by integer partitions.
For this reason, we now fix a notation for partitions and some associated objects.

Definition 6.7
.

The transpose of partition
\lambda=(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k})
is denoted
\lambda^{t}
and defined as

\lambda^{t}_{i}=\left\lvert\{j\in[k]\mid\lambda_{j}\geq i\}\right\rvert
.

Remark 6.8
.

For a partition
\lambda\vdash k
,
\lambda^{t}_{1}
is the number of rows/parts of
\lambda
.

Definition 6.9
.

The automorphism group of a partition
\aut(\lambda)\leq S_{\lambda^{t}_{1}}
is the group generated by transpositions
(i,j)

of rows
\lambda_{i}=\lambda_{j}
.

Remark 6.10
.

Let
\lambda\vdash k
and
p_{1}(\lambda),\dots,p_{k}(\lambda)
be such that
p_{i}(\lambda)=\left\lvert\{j\in[\lambda_{1}^{t}]\mid\lambda_{j}=i\}\right\rvert
.
Then
\aut(\lambda)\simeq S_{p_{1}}\times\cdots\times S_{p_{k}}
.

Lemma 6.11
.

We have

\sum_{\lambda\vdash k}\frac{\lambda!}{\lambda_{1}!\cdots\lambda_{k}!}\cdot\frac{(n)_{\lambda^{t}_{1}}}{\left\lvert\aut(\lambda)\right\rvert}\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\dots x_{k}^{\lambda_{k}}\right]=n^{k/2}.

Proof.

For
x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})
, we have
(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i})^{k}=n^{k/2}
. Then expanding
(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i})^{k}
in the previous equations and
taking the expectation over
\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})
on both sides
yields the result of the lemma (after appropriately collecting
terms).

Claim 6.12
.

Let
\lambda\vdash k
. We have

(n)_{\lambda^{t}_{1}}\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\dots x_{k}^{\lambda_{k}}\right]\right\rvert\leq 3^{k^{3}}\cdot n^{k/2}.

Proof.

We induct on
k
. For
k=1
, we have
n\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}\right]\right\rvert=\sqrt{n}\leq 3\cdot n^{1/2}
.
Now, suppose
k\geq 2
. We consider three cases:

1.

Case
\lambda_{1}\geq 3
: Let
\lambda^{\prime}
be the partition obtained from
\lambda
by removing two boxes from
\lambda_{1}
.
Note that
\lambda_{1}^{t}=(\lambda^{\prime})^{t}_{1}\leq k-2
and

{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}^{\prime}}\dots x_{k-2}^{\lambda_{k-2}^{\prime}}\right]={\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\dots x_{k-2}^{\lambda_{k-2}}\right]
.
By the induction hypothesis, we have
(n)_{(\lambda^{\prime})^{t}_{1}}\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}^{\prime}}\dots x_{k-2}^{\lambda_{k-2}^{\prime}}\right]\right\rvert\leq 3^{(k-2)^{2}}\cdot n^{(k-2)/2}
.

2.

Case
\lambda_{1}=2
: Let
\lambda^{\prime}
be the partition obtained from
\lambda
by removing
\lambda_{1}
.
Note that
\lambda_{1}^{t}=(\lambda^{\prime})^{t}_{1}+1\leq k-2
. By the induction hypothesis, we have

\displaystyle(n)_{\lambda^{t}_{1}}\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\dots x_{k-2}^{\lambda_{k-2}}\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq n\cdot(n)_{(\lambda^{\prime})^{t}_{1}}\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}^{\prime}}\dots x_{k-2}^{\lambda_{k-2}^{\prime}}\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 3^{(k-2)^{3}}\cdot n^{k/2}.

3.

Case
\lambda_{1}=1
: To bound
(n)_{k}\cdot{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\dots x_{k}^{\lambda_{k}}\right]
, we use
Lemma

6.11

and the two preceding cases. Let
p(k)
be the partition function, i.e.,
p(k)=\left\lvert\{\lambda\vdash k\}\right\rvert
. We deduce that

\displaystyle(n)_{k}\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\dots x_{k}^{\lambda_{k}}\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq n^{k/2}+\sum_{\lambda\vdash k\colon\lambda_{1}\geq 2}\frac{\lambda!}{\lambda_{1}!\cdots\lambda_{k}!}\cdot\frac{(n)_{\lambda^{t}_{1}}}{\left\lvert\aut(\lambda)\right\rvert}\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\dots x_{k}^{\lambda_{k}}\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq n^{k/2}+k!\sum_{\lambda\vdash k\colon\lambda_{1}\geq 2}(n)_{\lambda^{t}_{1}}\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\dots x_{k}^{\lambda_{k}}\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq n^{k/2}+k!\sum_{\lambda\vdash k\colon\lambda_{1}\geq 3}(n)_{\lambda^{t}_{1}}\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\dots x_{k}^{\lambda_{k}}\right]\right\rvert+

\displaystyle\hskip 17.00024pt\hskip 17.00024ptk!\sum_{\lambda\vdash k\colon\lambda_{1}=2}(n)_{\lambda^{t}_{1}}\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\dots x_{k}^{\lambda_{k}}\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq 3^{(k-2)^{3}}\cdot k!\cdot(1+p(k)+k)\cdot n^{k/2}\leq 3^{k^{3}}\cdot n^{k/2},

as desired.

Claim 6.13
.

Suppose
k<\sqrt{n}/2
. We have

\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}\dots x_{k}\right]\right\rvert\leq 2\cdot 3^{k^{3}}\cdot n^{-k/2}.

Proof.

Follows from
6.12
and the bound on
k
.

Remark 6.14
.

In
6.13
, the factor
3^{k^{3}}
is too lossy to
allow a meaningful bound with
k=n^{\varepsilon}
, where
\varepsilon>0
is a constant.

Refining the ideas of
6.12
, we prove a
stronger lemma below which will imply a tighter bound on
e(k)

sufficient for our application.

Lemma 6.15
.

There exists a universal constant
C\geq 1
such that

\sum_{\lambda\vdash k}\frac{\lambda!}{\lambda_{1}!\cdots\lambda_{k}!}\cdot\frac{(n)_{\lambda^{t}_{1}}}{\left\lvert\aut(\lambda)\right\rvert}\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\dots x_{k}^{\lambda_{k}}\right]\right\rvert\leq k^{C\cdot k}\cdot n^{k/2}.

(1)

In particular, for
n\geq 6
,
Eq.

1
holds with
C=2
.

Proof.

We induct on
k
. For
k=1
, we have
n\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\slice(\sqrt{n})}x_{1}\right\rvert\leq\sqrt{n}
as desired.
Using
e(2)=0
from
6.5
and the case
k=1
of
Eq.

1
, we get that
Lemma

6.15

also holds for
k=2
. Now, consider
k\geq 3
. Let
\Lambda_{1}=\{\lambda\vdash k\mid\lambda_{1}=1\}
,

\Lambda_{2}=\{\lambda\vdash k\mid\lambda_{1}=2\}
and
\Lambda_{\geq 3}=\{\lambda\vdash k\mid\lambda_{1}=3\}
.
Note that
\Lambda_{1}\sqcup\Lambda_{2}\sqcup\Lambda_{\geq 3}=\{\lambda\vdash k\}
and
\left\lvert\Lambda_{1}\right\rvert=1
.

For convenience define
a_{\lambda}
to be the term associated to
\lambda\vdash k
on the LHS of
Eq.

1
, i.e.,

a_{\lambda}=\frac{\lambda!}{\lambda_{1}!\cdots\lambda_{k}!}\cdot\frac{(n)_{\lambda^{t}_{1}}}{\left\lvert\aut(\lambda)\right\rvert}\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\dots x_{k}^{\lambda_{k}}\right]\right\rvert.

First we bound the contribution of the terms associated to
partitions from
\Lambda_{\geq 3}
in the LHS
of
Eq.

1
. Let
\lambda^{\prime}
be the partition obtained
from
\lambda
by removing two boxes from
\lambda_{1}
. Note that

\lambda_{1}^{t}=(\lambda^{\prime})^{t}_{1}\leq k-2
and

{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}^{\prime}}\dots x_{k-2}^{\lambda_{k-2}^{\prime}}\right]={\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\dots x_{k-2}^{\lambda_{k-2}}\right]
. Thus,

\displaystyle a_{\lambda}=\frac{\lambda!}{\lambda_{1}!\cdots\lambda_{k}!}\cdot\frac{(n)_{\lambda^{t}_{1}}}{\left\lvert\aut(\lambda)\right\rvert}\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\dots x_{k}^{\lambda_{k}}\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad=\frac{k(k-1)}{\lambda_{1}(\lambda_{1}-1)}\cdot\frac{\left\lvert\aut(\lambda^{\prime})\right\rvert}{\left\lvert\aut(\lambda)\right\rvert}\frac{\lambda^{\prime}!}{\lambda_{1}^{\prime}!\cdots\lambda_{k}^{\prime}!}\cdot\frac{(n)_{(\lambda^{\prime})^{t}_{1}}}{\left\lvert\aut(\lambda^{\prime})\right\rvert}\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}^{\prime}}\dots x_{k-2}^{\lambda_{k-2}^{\prime}}\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad=k^{2}\cdot\frac{\left\lvert\aut(\lambda^{\prime})\right\rvert}{\left\lvert\aut(\lambda)\right\rvert}\cdot a_{\lambda^{\prime}}\leq k^{3}\cdot a_{\lambda^{\prime}},

since
\left\lvert\aut(\lambda^{\prime})\right\rvert/\left\lvert\aut(\lambda)\right\rvert\leq k-2\leq k
.
For each
\lambda^{\prime}\vdash k-2
, we can form a partition
\lambda\vdash k
in
k-2\leq k

ways by adding two blocks to a single row of
\lambda^{\prime}
. Hence, we have

\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{\geq 3}}a_{\lambda}\leq k\cdot\sum_{\lambda^{\prime}\vdash k-2}k^{3}\cdot a_{\lambda^{\prime}}\leq k^{4}\cdot k^{C\cdot(k-2)}\cdot n^{(k-2)/2},

(2)

where the last equality follows from the induction hypothesis.

Now we bound the contribution of the terms
a_{\lambda}
associated to partitions
\lambda

from
\Lambda_{2}
in the LHS of
Eq.

1
. Let
i\geq 1

be the number of parts of size two of
\lambda

and let
\lambda^{\prime}
be the partition obtained
from
\lambda
by removing these
i
parts of size two. Note that
\lambda_{1}^{t}=(\lambda^{\prime})^{t}_{1}+i\leq k-1
. We have

\displaystyle a_{\lambda}=\frac{\lambda!}{\lambda_{1}!\cdots\lambda_{k}!}\cdot\frac{(n)_{\lambda^{t}_{1}}}{\left\lvert\aut(\lambda)\right\rvert}\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\dots x_{k}^{\lambda_{k}}\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad\leq n^{i}\cdot\frac{(k)_{i}}{2^{i}}\cdot\frac{\left\lvert\aut(\lambda^{\prime})\right\rvert}{\left\lvert\aut(\lambda)\right\rvert}\cdot\frac{\lambda^{\prime}!}{\lambda_{1}^{\prime}!\cdots\lambda_{k}^{\prime}!}\cdot\frac{(n)_{(\lambda^{\prime})^{t}_{1}}}{\left\lvert\aut(\lambda^{\prime})\right\rvert}\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}\dots x_{k-2i}\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\qquad=n^{i}\cdot\frac{(k)_{i}}{2^{i}}\cdot\frac{1}{i!}\cdot\frac{\lambda^{\prime}!}{\lambda_{1}^{\prime}!\cdots\lambda_{k}^{\prime}!}\cdot\frac{(n)_{(\lambda^{\prime})^{t}_{1}}}{\left\lvert\aut(\lambda^{\prime})\right\rvert}\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}\dots x_{k-2i}\right]\right\rvert,

where in the last equality we used
\left\lvert\aut(\lambda^{\prime})\right\rvert/\left\lvert\aut(\lambda)\right\rvert=1/(i!)
.
Since
\lambda\in\Lambda_{2}
is uniquely specified by its number of parts of size two, applying the induction hypothesis we have

\displaystyle\sum_{\lambda\in\Lambda_{2}}a_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}n^{i}\cdot\frac{(k)_{i}}{2^{i}}\cdot\frac{1}{i!}\cdot\left(\frac{\lambda^{\prime}!}{\lambda_{1}^{\prime}!\cdots\lambda_{k}^{\prime}!}\cdot\frac{(n)_{(\lambda^{\prime})^{t}_{1}}}{\left\lvert\aut(\lambda^{\prime})\right\rvert}\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}\dots x_{k-2i}\right]\right\rvert\right)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{i=1}^{\lfloor k/2\rfloor}n^{i}\cdot\frac{(k)_{i}}{2^{i}}\cdot\frac{1}{i!}\cdot k^{C\cdot(k-2i)}\cdot n^{(k-2i)/2}

\displaystyle\leq k^{C\cdot(k-1)}\cdot n^{k/2}\cdot\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}k^{-C\cdot i}\leq\frac{3}{2}\cdot k^{C\cdot(k-1)}\cdot n^{k/2},

where in the last inequality we used
k\geq 3
and
C\geq 1
.

Finally, we consider the case
\lambda_{1}=1
. To bound
a_{\lambda}
, we use
Lemma

6.11

and the two preceding cases. We deduce that

\displaystyle a_{\lambda}

\displaystyle\leq n^{k/2}+\sum_{\mu\in\Lambda_{2}}a_{\mu}+\sum_{\mu\in\Lambda_{\geq 3}}a_{\mu}\leq n^{k/2}+k^{4}\cdot k^{C\cdot(k-2)}\cdot n^{(k-2)/2}+\frac{3}{2}\cdot k^{C\cdot(k-1)}\cdot n^{k/2}

\displaystyle=k^{C\cdot k}\cdot n^{k/2}\left(\frac{1}{k^{C\cdot k}}+\frac{k^{4}}{n\cdot k^{2\cdot C}}+\frac{3}{2\cdot k^{C}}\right).

We can bound the LHS of
Eq.

1
as

\displaystyle\sum_{\mu\in\Lambda_{1}}a_{\mu}+\sum_{\mu\in\Lambda_{2}}a_{\mu}+\sum_{\mu\in\Lambda_{\geq 3}}a_{\mu}

\displaystyle\leq k^{C\cdot k}\cdot n^{k/2}\left(\frac{1}{k^{C\cdot k}}+\frac{2\cdot k^{4}}{n\cdot k^{2\cdot C}}+\frac{3}{k^{C}}\right)

\displaystyle\leq k^{C\cdot k}\cdot n^{k/2},

provided
C>0
is a sufficiently large constant. In particular, the constant
C
can be taken to be
2
for
n\geq 6
.

Corollary 6.16
.

We have

\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}\dots x_{k}\right]\right\rvert\leq k^{3\cdot k}\cdot n^{-k/2}.

Proof.

Suppose
k\leq\sqrt{n}
.
Note that
Lemma

6.15
implies that for
\lambda\vdash k
with
\lambda_{1}

there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\frac{\lambda!}{\lambda_{1}!\cdots\lambda_{k}!}\cdot\frac{(n)_{\lambda^{t}_{1}}}{\left\lvert\aut(\lambda)\right\rvert}\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}^{\lambda_{1}}\dots x_{k}^{\lambda_{k}}\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle=(n)_{k}\cdot\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}\dots x_{k}\right]\right\rvert

\displaystyle\leq k^{C\cdot k}\cdot n^{k/2}.

Simplifying and using the assumption
k\leq\sqrt{n}
, we obtain

\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}\dots x_{k}\right]\right\rvert\leq\frac{k^{C\cdot k}\cdot n^{-k/2}}{\prod_{i=1}^{k-1}\left(1-\frac{i}{n}\right)}\leq 2\cdot k^{C\cdot k}\cdot n^{-k/2}.

Furthermore, for
n\geq 6
,
Lemma

6.15
allows us to choose
C=2
.
Since
\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}\right]\right\rvert=1/\sqrt{n}
, the simpler
bound applies for all values of
k

\left\lvert{\mathbb{E}}_{x\in\mathcal{S}(\sqrt{n})}\left[x_{1}\dots x_{k}\right]\right\rvert\leq k^{3\cdot k}\cdot n^{-k/2},

Now the assumption
n\geq 6
can be removed since, for
k\geq 2
, we have
(k^{3}/\sqrt{n})^{k}\geq 1
,
where
1
is the trivial bound. Similarly, our initial assumption of
k\leq\sqrt{n}
can also be removed
as the bound also becomes trivial in the regime
k>\sqrt{n}
.

Chapter 5
The machinery and Qualitative bounds

In this chapter, we first state the main machinery that we use to prove our results. The machinery is a meta theorem that shows that under several linear algebraic conditions, a large random matrix is positive semidefinite (PSD) with high probability. This is similar in spirit to the PSDness argument in the SoS lower bounds for the Sherrington-Kirkpatrick Hamiltonian from the last chapter, although it’s quite a bit more involved.

The machinery originally appeared in
[
potechin2020machinery
,
potechin2022sub
]
, where the complete proof can be found. In
Section

1
, we state the machinery. Compared to that work, we significantly simplify the required definitions needed to state and apply the machinery. Such a decluttering of the definitions is possible since we don’t provide the proof and simply apply the theorem. For example, we don’t define ribbons, we don’t formally define the technical matrix
M^{orth}_{\tau}(H)
and we work in the simplified setting of Rademacher or Gaussian variables instead of arbitrary distributions with finite moments. Moreover, we interpret the conditions of the machinery as conditions of the problem itself, rather than computational linear algebraic conditions on the moment matrix as in
[
potechin2020machinery
]
. Cast in this framework, this makes clear the potential connection to the low degree likelihood ratio hypothesis as described in
Chapter

2
and
Chapter

7
.

After stating the machinery in
Section

1
, we exhibit the qualitative bounds for applying the machinery to our problems of interest. The material in these sections is also adapted from
[
potechin2020machinery
]
. The main difference is that we improve the exposition by fixing various typos and clarifying various technical arguments.

1
The machinery

In this section, we describe the machinery we apply to show SoS lower bounds.
As we have already seen in
Chapter

4
, the general idea to show SoS lower bounds in this work is to decompose the moment matrix into graph matrices, which are matrix-valued functions of the input entries, and then show PSDness by exhibiting an approximate PSD decomposition. The machinery takes a similar approach and provides an approximate PSD decomposition, using certain decay properties of the Fourier coefficients as well as norm bounds similar to the ones obtained in
Chapter

1
.

Consider a hypothesis testing problem
{\mathcal{P}}
. We will assume the setup in
Section

3
. Therefore,
{\mathcal{P}}
is a distinguishing problem between two distributions – the random distribution (null hypothesis) and the planted distribution (alternative hypothesis). As we saw earlier, we could use the technique of pseudo-calibration to obtain a candidate moment matrix
\Lambda
, such that
\Lambda
can potentially serve as an SoS lower bound for the related optimization task on the random distribution. The machinery gives general conditions on
\Lambda
that ensure feasibility with high probability. In particular, the machinery is a set of linear algebraic conditions on
\Lambda
that imply positivity of
\Lambda
w.h.p. and as we saw earlier, the other required feasibility conditions follow easily from pseudo-calibration.

In this work, we slightly diverge from this viewpoint (originally presented in
[
potechin2020machinery
]
) and instead view these conditions as properties of the problem
{\mathcal{P}}
directly. Therefore, the machinery can be construed as a claim of feasibility of the pseudo-cailbrated pseudo-expectation operator, under certain conditions on the problem
{\mathcal{P}}
. To state the machinery in this language, we need some definitions that follow next.

1
Shapes and graph matrices

Consider the setting when the input distribution is a Rademacher
G_{n,1/2}
graph with the input entries being
\chi_{e}\in\{-1,1\}
. For
T\subseteq\binom{[n]}{2}
, let
\chi_{T}=\prod_{e\in T}\chi_{e}
be the standard Fourier basis. In this setting, shapes were already defined in
Chapter

1
. Here, for technical reasons, we slightly modify the definitions so that the rows and columns are indexed by sub-tuples of
[n]
rather than subsets of
[n]
. The techniques developed in
Chapter

1
still carry over to bound the norms of such graph matrices.

Definition 1.1

(Shapes in the setting of Rademacher
G_{n,1/2}
inputs)
.

A shape
\alpha=(V(\alpha),E(\alpha),U_{\alpha},V_{\alpha})
is a graph on vertices
V(\alpha)
and edges
E(\alpha)
with two distinguished tuples of vertices
U_{\alpha},V_{\alpha}\subseteq V(\alpha)
. Note that
U_{\alpha},V_{\alpha}
are ordered subsets (tuples).

As we saw earlier, we can define corresponding matrices for each shape, that are termed graph matrices. Recall that a realization is an injective map from
V(\alpha)
to
[n]
. The main difference here, as compared to
Chapter

1
, is that in the definition of graph matrices, we sum over realizations
\varphi
that correspond to distinct characters, rather than all realizations
\varphi
.

To capture this notion precisely, we use the following definition. Define two realizations (injective maps from
V(\alpha)
to
[n]
)
\varphi,\varphi^{\prime}
to be equivalent if
\varphi(U_{\alpha})=\varphi^{\prime}(U_{\alpha}),\varphi(V_{\alpha})=\varphi^{\prime}(V_{\alpha})
as tuples and
\varphi(E(\alpha))=\varphi^{\prime}(E(\alpha))
as sets. Let the set of non-equivalent realizations of
\alpha
be denoted
\mathrm{Real}(\alpha)
.

Definition 1.2

(Graph matrices in the setting of Rademacher
G_{n,1/2}
inputs)
.

For a shape
\alpha
, the graph matrix
M_{\alpha}
is a matrix-valued function with rows and columns indexed by sub-tuples of
[n]
of sizes
|U_{\alpha}|,|V_{\alpha}|
respectively, which is defined as follows: It maps input graph
G\in\{\pm 1\}^{\binom{n}{2}}
(wich associated fourier characters
\chi_{E}
) to a matrix with the
A,B
-th entry being

M_{\alpha}(A,B)=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}\varphi(U_{\alpha})=A,\varphi(V_{\alpha})=B\\
\varphi\in\mathrm{Real}(\alpha)\end{subarray}}{\chi_{E(\alpha)}}

Definition 1.3

(Shape transposes)
.

For a shape
\alpha=(V(\alpha),E(\alpha),U_{\alpha},V_{\alpha})
, define its transpose
\alpha^{T}
to be
\alpha^{T}=(V(\alpha),E(\alpha),V_{\alpha},U_{\alpha})
.
Note that
M_{\alpha^{T}}=M_{\alpha}^{T}
as matrix transpose.

Example 1.4
.

In
Fig.

1
, consider the shape
\alpha
as shown. We have
U_{\alpha}=(u_{1},u_{2}),V_{\alpha}=(v_{1}),V(\alpha)=\{u_{1},u_{2},v_{1},w_{1}\}
and
E(\alpha)=\{\{u_{1},w_{1}\},\{u_{2},w_{1}\},\{w_{1},v_{1}\}\}
.
M_{\alpha}
is a matrix with rows and columns indexed by tuples of length
|U_{\alpha}|=2
and
|V_{\alpha}|=1
respectively. The nonzero entries will have rows and columns indexed by
(a_{1},a_{2})
and
b_{1}
respectively for all distinct
a_{1},a_{2},b_{1}
, with the corresponding entry being
M_{\alpha}((a_{1},a_{2}),(b_{1}))=\sum_{c_{1}\in[n]\setminus\{a_{1},a_{2},b_{1}\}}\chi_{a_{1},c_{1}}\chi_{a_{2},c_{1}}\chi_{c_{1},b_{1}}
. Here, the injective map
\varphi
maps the vertices
u_{1},u_{2},w_{1},v_{1}
to
a_{1},a_{2},c_{1},b_{1}
respectively and we sum over all such maps (as they are all pairwise non-equivalent). Succinctly,

M_{\alpha}=\begin{array}[]{rl@{}c@{}r}&&\makebox[0.0pt]{column $(b_{1})$}\\[-2.15277pt]
&&\,\downarrow\\[-2.15277pt]
&&\vdots&\\[-0.86108pt]
\text{row }(a_{1},a_{2})\rightarrow&\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\makebox[32.71089pt][l]{\leaders{\hbox{.}}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}}&\sum_{c_{1}\in[n]\setminus\{a_{1},a_{2},b_{1}\}}\chi_{a_{1},c_{1}}\chi_{a_{2},c_{1}}\chi_{c_{1},b_{1}}&\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\makebox[42.93306pt][r]{\leaders{\hbox{.}}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}}\\[2.15277pt]
&&\vdots&\\
\end{array}

Figure 1
:
Example of a shape

Some simple matrices such as the adjacency matrix of a graph and the identity matrix are also graph matrices, as we see below

•

Take
\alpha
to be a shape with two vertices
u,v
with
U_{\alpha}=(u),V_{\alpha}=(v)
and exactly one edge
\{u,v\}
. Then,
M_{\alpha}
has rows and columns indexed by
[n]
(more specifically tuples of length
1
) with the
i,j
-th entry being
G_{ij}
if
i\neq j
and
0
otherwise. Therefore,
M_{\alpha}
is just the
\pm 1
adjacency matrix of the graph
G
.

•

Take
\alpha
to be the shape with exactly
1
vertex
u
, no edges and
U_{\alpha}=V_{\alpha}=(u)
. Then,
M_{\alpha}
is the identity matrix of size
n\times n
.

For more examples of graph matrices and why they can be a useful tool to work with, see
[
ahn2016graph
]
.
We now define some terms to capture the rows and columns of graph matrices.

Matrix indices and index shapes

In the above setting of Rademacher
G_{n,1/2}
inputs, a matrix index
A
is a tuple of indices
(a_{1},\ldots,a_{|A|})
where
a_{i}\in[n]
. When the SoS variables are
y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}
, we associate to this matrix index
A
the monomial
\prod_{i\leq|A|}y_{a_{i}}
. With this definition, graph matrices have as rows and columns matrix indices.

Define an index shape
U=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{|U|})
to be a tuple of formal variables
u_{i}
, or in other words, unspecified indices. If
|U|=t
, we say that any matrix index
A
of length
t
has shape
U
. We say two index shapes
U,V
are equivalent, denoted
U\equiv V
if
|U|=|V|
. Finally, define the weight of
U
to be
w(U)=|U|
and the automorphism group
Aut(U)=S_{|U|}
(the permutations of the elements of
U
). The latter definition is needed for describing coefficients.

Shape definitions

We say a shape
\alpha
is proper if it has no isolated vertices (i.e. no degree
0
vertices) outside
U_{\alpha}\cup V_{\alpha}
. We say a shape
\alpha
is trivial if
U_{\alpha}
and
V_{\alpha}
are equal as sets, and they constitute all the vertices in
\alpha
. and moreover, there are no edges in
\alpha
.

A path is a sequence of vertices of
V(\alpha)
such that every consecutive pair of vertices form an edge in
V(\alpha)
. A vertex separator of
\alpha
is a set of vertices
S
such that every path from
U_{\alpha}
to
V_{\alpha}
passes through
S
. As we saw in
Chapter

1
, the norm bounds of the graph matrix
M_{\alpha}
rely on the size of the minimum vertex separator of
\alpha
. Define the weight of a vertex separator
S
as
|S|
.

The above definitions are sufficient for the application to the Planted Slightly Denser subgraph problem. But when we work with Tensor PCA and Sparse PCA, we need to generalize the notion of shapes and graph matrices. These generalized shapes and graph matrices were studied in
[
ahn2016graph
]
. Now, we describe the required generalizations.

Definitions for Tensor PCA

In the Tensor PCA application, the input is a tensor
A\in\mathbb{R}^{[n]^{k}}
. To incorporate this, we modify our definitions of shapes and index shapes accordingly. The input entries are now sampled from the distribution
\mathcal{N}(0,1)
instead of
\{-1,1\}
. So, we will work with the Hermite basis of polynomials.
Let the standard unnormalized Hermite polynomials be denoted as
h_{0}(x)=1,h_{1}(x)=x,h_{2}(x)=x^{2}-1,\ldots
